The app never renders its own skeletons - `ui/course_selector.py` only styles around Streamlit's, having hit the same thing from the other side.
- **The valve must wait on a script that is still RUNNING - slow is not hung.** Measured: a sync-page render stalled **14.7 s** behind an expired `fetch_courses` cache; the old unconditional 8 s valve uncovered onto a frozen half-built page and left it there for the remaining 8.5 s. A `running` state is positive evidence the server is alive, so `VALVE` now applies only once the script is idle, with `HARD_VALVE` (30 s) as the absolute cap - because a mask nothing can lift is the one failure a user cannot work around.
- **`SHOW_DELAY` is 0, and it INVERTED - do not "restore" the deferral without re-measuring.** It was 200 ms, on the sound reasoning that a navigation's first DOM change did not land until 124-235 ms, so an earlier mask covered a page that had not moved. That was true *of an app whose pages took 1.2-1.9 s to render*. Once `core/course_cache.py` took the Canvas fetch off the render path and whole navigations began finishing in 170-700 ms, the churn started almost immediately and 200 ms stopped arriving early - it arrived **after most of the swap**. The deferral machinery is still there; only the constant changed, so re-tuning stays a one-line job.
- **Measure it with `exposed_churn_ms`, not `leak_ms`.** `leak_ms` counts only the *translucent* window and made the problem look small (median 12 ms). `exposed_churn_ms` counts every millisecond the page is changing while the mask is below 85 % opacity - un-painted, mid-fade or already lifted - and that is what the user sees. At `SHOW_DELAY=200` it was **median 311 ms, max 458, on EVERY transition**, reported as *"through each transition it looks like the application is severely glitching - elements are being scrambled everywhere"*. A DOM-signature settle time cannot find this; it is a question about what is on screen, not about when the DOM stopped moving.
- **Paint SYNCHRONOUSLY from the click handler when `SHOW_DELAY <= 0`.** A `setTimeout(0)` is still a macrotask hop and measured ~34 ms before the mask reached full strength - which was **100 % of the remaining exposure** (nothing leaks mid-mask or after it). Inline painting puts the mask in the DOM before the browser's next frame. Final: **exposed churn mean 53 ms, median 54, max 69**, 0/30 transitions unmasked, 0/30 premature reveals - against 351/372/617 originally.
- The cost of covering everything is that **every** navigation now shows the mask (median ~600 ms visible, 372-1514 ms). That is only affordable because the two fixes above made the thing being hidden short; at the original 1.2-1.9 s page times it would have been intolerable.
- **`opacity` transitions, `display` does not.** A photo finish then costs a few percent of opacity instead of a black flash, and `p.vis` + `pointer-events` drop at the *start* of the fade so the page is live throughout.
- **The fade-IN is the one thing that can make the mask worse than no mask**, and it must stay short and front-loaded. While it ramps, the page churns visibly *through* it - reported as "the ui shift is visible for a split second behind it at ~50% opacity". `scripts/measure_nav.py` measures it as **`leak_ms`**: time the mask is translucent (0.02-0.85) while the visual signature is still changing. A 150 ms **linear** fade-in leaked a median 138-155 ms on the download-settings screens, **258 ms** at worst, on 18 of 30 transitions. Now 70 ms on `cubic-bezier(.2,.8,.3,1)`, which is past 85 % opacity in about the first third - where the whole leak is paid. Do not lengthen it or make it linear again: the thing a slow fade protects against (a transition finishing just after the paint, flashing at full strength) is rare, because `paint()`'s evidence check already declines the fast ones. The fade-OUT can stay leisurely - by then the page is final, so there is nothing left to leak. `data-stale` as a coherence gate is **provably redundant** - `isElementStale` in the 1.51 bundle returns `false` whenever the state is not `running`/`rerunRequested`, so it can never add information to the script-state check.
### The course list must never be fetched on the render path
- Once the overlay stopped hiding it, fetching the course list was the largest remaining page-render blocker. `core/course_cache.py` owns it now. Measured end to end: **click -> page usable went from 2046 ms to 487 ms (median), and 11 of 30 navigations stopped showing an overlay at all** because the page now arrives inside the mask's 200 ms deferral.
- **The blanket `st.cache_resource.clear()` was the whole problem.** `cleanup_download_state()` and `cleanup_sync_state()` each called it, and the **sidebar nav buttons call those on every mode switch** - so every navigation threw the course list away and the next page paid ~950 ms of Canvas round-trips. That is the entire reason "-> Today" was the only fast navigation in the app: it is the one screen that needs no course list. Sidebar navigations improved **2.3-4.4x** on the page side from removing it. Nothing about finishing a download changes which courses you are enrolled in; the two cases that genuinely need fresh data clear it explicitly (logout in `ui/auth.py`, the Refresh button in `ui/course_selector.py`) and both still work because `clear` is attached to the function.
- **`include[]=favorites` answers in ONE request what took three.** `canvasapi`'s `get_current_user()` eagerly does `GET /users/self` before it will hand over `get_favorite_courses()`, so the obvious implementation costs `/courses` + `/users/self` + `/favorites/courses` = **950 ms measured**. `CanvasManager.get_courses_with_favorites()` asks Canvas to include the flag: **432 ms, one request**, and the courses it flagged matched the favorites endpoint exactly (15 of 33, identical ids). Canvas **silently ignores** an unknown `include[]` value rather than erroring, so the result is *verified* (`all(hasattr(c, 'is_favorite') ...)`) and the three-call path stays as the fallback.
- **`st.cache_resource` cannot express what is wanted here**, which is why the cache is hand-rolled: its TTL **expires** rather than refreshes, so one navigation every ten minutes still paid full price; and it cannot be populated from a background thread, which has no `ScriptRunContext`. The rule is fresh -> serve, stale -> serve **and** refresh behind it, absent/ancient -> block. Concurrent cold fetches are deduped through an in-flight registry, and a failed refresh keeps the working list and backs off rather than retrying on every rerun.
- Nothing in `core/course_cache.py` may touch `st.*` - `_load_courses` runs on a plain daemon thread. `CanvasManager.__init__` and `get_courses_with_favorites` were both checked to be Streamlit-free.

- Server-side render time is a **separate** cost the mask was hiding, and it is now the dominant one: `sync1->dl1` and `today->sync1` genuinely take 1.1-1.7 s of Python. `fetch_courses` is `@st.cache_resource(ttl=600)`, so the first navigation to the sync or download page after 10 idle minutes pays two Canvas round-trips inside the render - observed as a frozen mid-render gap of **1.9 s typically and 14.7 s at worst**. Do not read those as overlay latency; the overlay is now within ~180 ms of the truth on every one of them.

### Never wrap the whole page in `st.empty().container()` (Critical)
- `app.py` used to render every screen inside `_main_content = st.empty()` / `with _main_content.container():`. Both deltas land on ONE delta path and `ForwardMsgQueue.enqueue` composes them, so the `Empty` normally never ships - **but composition only holds while both are still in the queue, and `Runtime._loop_coroutine` flushes every ~10ms.** Lose that race and the browser renders an empty box **in place of the entire page** until the container delta arrives.
- Reported as *"after a certain amount of clicks the whole page turns dark for ~10ms and then comes back straight, with no UI shifting"*. Reproduced 2026-07-31 by driving **300 rapid reruns**: the page blanked **four times (~1.3% of reruns)**, ~20ms each. Captured mid-dip: `stMain` down to **5-6 element containers, every one height 0**, `innerText` empty, and **exactly one `stEmpty` present** - while `stMain`'s own height stayed 999px, which is precisely why the content returns without shifting anything.
- **It bought nothing.** The wrapper existed so a long screen could wipe the page mid-run via `main_placeholder.empty()`, but `render_sync_step4()` is called with **no arguments**, so `sync/analysis.py`'s "wipe before blocking on analysis" had been a **no-op for its whole life**; the only two live callers blanked the page ~200ms before an `st.rerun()` replaced it anyway. Leaving the old screen up until the new one arrives is the smoother of the two.
- A plain `st.container()` emits exactly ONE `vertical` block at the same index, so nothing about the page's reconciliation shape changes - only the stray `Empty` is gone. Verified with the identical 300-click storm: minimum element count **50**, **0 dips**.
- **Diagnostic worth reusing**: rAF-sample `stMain.querySelectorAll('[data-testid="stElementContainer"]').length` and flag any frame below half the baseline. A MutationObserver watching for an `stEmpty` node does NOT catch this (the surviving nodes are ordinary containers) - count elements, and capture `innerText` + the `stEmpty` count at the dip.

### A back-filled `st.empty()` UNMOUNTS its slot for the whole run (Critical)
- `st.empty()` is not a reservation, it is an **element**: it enqueues an `Empty` delta immediately, and `Empty` renders as `<div class="stEmpty">` — an empty box. Fill it later in the same run and the browser sees **two** frames, `real → Empty → real`, not one.
- **The composition rule does not save you across a gap.** `ForwardMsgQueue.enqueue` composes two deltas at one path into the last one, but only while both are still *in the queue*, and `Runtime._loop_coroutine` flushes every ~10ms. `slot = st.empty(); slot.container(...)` on adjacent lines composes and the `Empty` never ships (which is why the `_list_slot` spinner swap is atomic). Fill the slot after a few hundred elements and it ships on its own.
- Measured on the course-selector toolbar, whose live "x of y selected" count was an `st.empty()` filled at the end of the fragment so it could report the post-click selection:
  ```
  t=6302.3  REMOVED  stMarkdown "2 of 33 selected"
  t=6309.7  ADDED    stEmpty                        ← 76ms of reflowed toolbar
  t=6378.0  REMOVED  stEmpty
  t=6384.9  ADDED    stMarkdown "3 of 33 selected"
  ```
  The count is a flex item, so the search field and Refresh button slid **106px** left and back (764 → 658) on **every** checkbox click and every keystroke in the search box — one full cycle per letter typed.
- **The fix is to know the value before you render, not to reserve a slot.** Streamlit applies widget state to `st.session_state` *before* the script body runs, so anything derived from widgets is already knowable at the top of the run. `ui/course_selector.py:resolve_multi_selection()` is that derivation for the course list, and it is deliberately ONE function called by both the toolbar (up front) and `_render_multi_select_list` (as it renders) — writing the reconciliation rule twice is how the label and the list would drift apart.
- Related, same file, same fix: `Select All` / `Clear Selection` / `Refresh` are `on_click=` callbacks. `if st.button(): mutate; st.rerun()` renders the fragment **twice** and streams the pre-click count and list in between.
- **A label whose text shrinks still moves its neighbours.** After the placeholder was gone, "0 of 33" → "0 of 6" was still worth 7px of slide per keystroke. The count is a one-cell CSS grid holding the live label plus a `visibility: hidden` ghost sized from `all_courses` (the widest either number can get, constant until a re-fetch), so the box has exactly one width. The ghost carries the `<b>` too — bold digits are wider, and a plain-text sizer left a 0.9px twitch.
- Verified in the real app: 2,214 frames across typing / backspacing / rapid-clicking / Select All / Clear / view-toggle → 0 absent frames, 0 element swaps, and count width, search X and Refresh X each hold a **single** sub-pixel value throughout.

### SVG Icons in CSS and HTML

**SVG icons in expander labels and button labels**: Streamlit's `st.expander(label)` and `st.button(label)` accept plain text/markdown only - no HTML or SVG. To replace an emoji with an SVG icon:
1. Remove the emoji from the Python label string entirely.
2. Inject CSS `::before` on the label's rendered `<p>` element using a URL-encoded SVG data URI as `background-image`. Always use `content: ""` (empty string), not `content: url(...)` - the latter can't be sized.
3. Add `display: flex !important; align-items: center !important;` on the `<p>` so the pseudo-element and text sit on the same baseline.
4. **Specificity ordering for multiple container scopes**: A broad rule (e.g. `div[data-testid="stDialog"] ... summary p::before`) applies to all dialogs by default. More specific container-key selectors (e.g. `div[class*="st-key-preset_card_"] ... summary p::before`) naturally override it without needing `!important` tricks, as long as the specific rule has more attribute selectors. Put the broad rule first (earlier in CSS), specific overrides later.
5. The Streamlit expander label `<p>` lives at: `div[data-testid="stButton"] button p` (and for expanders: `div[data-testid="stExpander"] details summary p`).

**Vertically centring an icon + text label inside a button (Critical recipe)**: When you add a `::before` icon to a text button (e.g. a folder glyph left of "Open Folder"), the content visibly sits ABOVE the button's true centre unless you flex the **whole chain**. Flexing only the `<p>` is NOT enough - the `<p>` lives inside `[data-testid="stMarkdownContainer"]`, which is the button's actual flex child, so the container itself stays uncentred. The exact, proven recipe (see `inject_file_action_css`'s Open Folder block in `ui_shared.py` and the `pan_open_dialog_btn` block in `ui/download_settings.py`):
   1. `... button { align-items: center !important; justify-content: center !important; }` - centre the button's own content.
   2. `... button [data-testid="stMarkdownContainer"] { display: flex !important; align-items: center !important; }` - **the missing piece**: centre the label block (the real flex child).
   3. `... button p { display: inline-flex !important; align-items: center !important; gap: 8px !important; margin: 0 !important; }` - put icon + text on one centred line. The `margin: 0` is **required**: the label `<p>`'s default bottom margin is part of the flex item's margin-box, so `align-items: center` centres the margin-box and the visible content is pushed UP by half that margin. Symptom: content sits slightly high.
   4. **Do NOT set `line-height: 1`** on the `<p>`. It shrinks the line box below the glyph height, so the text overflows its own box and reads as mis-centred (visible in a box-model overlay as glyphs poking out the top of the text's box). Leave line-height at its default.
   5. `::before` icon: `content: ""; display: inline-block; width/height; flex-shrink: 0; background-image/size/position`. For grey-at-rest → white-on-hover, define two data-URI variants (e.g. `fill='%23b1bac4'` and `fill='%23ffffff'`) and swap via `... button:hover p::before { background-image: url(...) }`.
   6. Scope the whole block to the button's `key` prefix (`div[class*="st-key-<prefix>_open_"]`) so it never bleeds onto the per-file `fileact_open_` icon buttons that share the substring `_open_`.

**URL-encoded data URIs** (for CSS `background-image`, `<img src="...">`):
- Replace `<` → `%3C`, `>` → `%3E`, `#` → `%23` in the SVG source.
- Use single quotes inside the SVG attributes (not double quotes) to avoid breaking the outer CSS string.
- Solid/filled icon: `fill='%23hexcolor'` with no stroke.
- Outlined icon: `fill='none' stroke='%23hexcolor' stroke-width='2' stroke-linecap='round' stroke-linejoin='round'`.

**Inline SVGs** (for `st.markdown(unsafe_allow_html=True)` HTML strings):
- Use raw `<svg>` tags directly - no URL encoding needed.
- Can use CSS classes (`class='my-chevron'`) and `stroke='currentColor'` to inherit parent `color`.
- CSS can override SVG attributes (`stroke`, `stroke-width`, `fill`) on inline SVGs targeting the element's class.

### `st.html()` Shadow Root Behaviour (Extended)
- `st.html()` renders into a shadow root - CSS inside it is completely isolated from the main page, and main-page CSS cannot reach into it.
- Elements rendered by `st.markdown(unsafe_allow_html=True)` ARE in the main DOM and ARE reachable by external CSS selectors.
- Use `st.html()` ONLY for injecting `<style>` tags or zero-content spacers. Use `st.markdown(unsafe_allow_html=True)` for actual HTML content that needs to be styled by external CSS.

### `components.html` JS Bridges (`window.parent` DOM manipulation)

When pure CSS/Python can't express an interaction (Shift-click range select, live-as-you-type filtering, etc.), inject a same-origin `<script>` via `st.components.v1.html(..., height=0)` that reaches into `window.parent.document`. Hard-won rules:

- **The iframe is rebuilt when the srcdoc CHANGES, not on every rerun - so a bridge may never run again (Critical, measured 2026-07-31).** This file used to state flatly that `components.html` "builds a fresh iframe on every rerun and destroys the previous one". Tagging the live `<iframe>` and clicking through reruns shows Streamlit **reuses** it whenever the srcdoc is unchanged - and a bridge whose script is a constant string has, by construction, an unchanging srcdoc. The realistic model is: **the script runs once per mount, and you get no say in when the next mount is.**
  - Both halves matter, and they pull in opposite directions. The re-bind rule below is still right - the iframe *is* destroyed when it does remount. What is **not** right is assuming the script gets a chance to re-run each rerun. Anything that must stay true as the DOM changes has to be maintained by something that survives: a delegated listener on `document`, or an observer on `stMain`/`body` with `subtree: true` that re-derives from scratch when it fires.
  - **The one bridge that got this wrong took a one-shot MEASUREMENT and observed specific, replaceable nodes** - `sync_ui._inject_shist_height_bridge`, deleted 2026-07-31. React replaced button nodes underneath it, their inline heights went with them, and nothing ever ran again: sync-history cards rendered 62px tall around 106px headers, slicing the course pills in half and dropping the "Synced N files" line entirely. It failed *partially*, which is why it read as random - a ResizeObserver rescued the cards whose header node Streamlit mutated in place rather than replaced. Every other bridge in the app was audited the same day and is safe on this point: they delegate on `document` or observe an `stMain`/`body` subtree.
  - Prefer no bridge at all. That one existed to answer "how tall is this element", which CSS answers natively - see the one-cell grid recipe in Containers & Keys.
- **NEVER use a one-time `if (st.bound) return;` guard to attach a listener once for the page lifetime.** When the iframe *is* rebuilt it destroys the previous one, and a listener attached from inside a destroyed iframe's JS realm **silently stops firing** (the realm/closure is dead). With a one-time guard, the listener is never re-attached, so the feature works on first load and then **dies permanently after the first iframe teardown** - most visibly after a *full-page* rerun (e.g. a segmented toggle outside the fragment). Symptom: "works when I refresh, but once it stops it never recovers, and manual blur/click still works."
- **Correct pattern: re-bind a fresh listener on EVERY injection.** The bridge function is called at the end of the fragment, so it re-runs on every fragment + full-page rerun. Each run: (1) remove the previous listener (`doc.removeEventListener('evt', reg.handler, true)` inside a `try/catch` - the stored ref stays valid for removal even if its realm is dead), (2) create a fresh handler closure (alive realm), (3) `addEventListener` it, (4) stash it on `reg.handler`. A blur/click that commits triggers a rerun → re-injection → fresh handler, so there's always a live listener.
- **Persist mutable state on `window.parent`, NOT in the iframe closure.** Store `{handler, timer, last, anchorKey, applying, ...}` on a `window.parent._cdXxx` registry object so it survives across re-binds (the iframe closure is recreated/destroyed every rerun). Use `window.parent.setTimeout`/`clearTimeout` so timer ids stay valid no matter which realm clears them.
- **Use event delegation on `document`** (capture phase where order matters) and re-query the live node set on each event, rather than binding to specific widget nodes that Streamlit reconciles/replaces.
- **Committing a text input live (filter-as-you-type):** Streamlit only commits a text input's value to Python on **blur or Enter**. A *synthetic* `Enter` `KeyboardEvent` is **ignored** (untrusted key events don't trigger React's commit). A programmatic **`inp.blur()` reliably fires the same onBlur commit as clicking out**; immediately `inp.focus({preventScroll:true})` and restore the caret with `inp.setSelectionRange(start,end)` so typing continues uninterrupted. Debounce (~180-200ms) and guard with a `last`-committed-value check to keep reruns to ~one per typing pause. (See `inject_search_live_bridge` and `inject_shift_select_bridge` in `ui/course_selector.py` for the canonical implementations.)
  - **`blur()` is a NO-OP on an element that is not focused, so a blur-based commit silently does nothing whenever focus has moved on (Critical).** It is invisible in review because the *typing* path always holds focus - the failure only appears on a commit the user triggers from somewhere else. Measured 2026-07-27 on the clear-search X: type a query → click the favorites/all pill (full rerun, focus lands on the pill, field re-renders unfocused) → click the X. The DOM value cleared and React went dirty, but `inp.blur()` did nothing, so the query never reached Python and **the course list kept the old filter until the user clicked anywhere else** and that real blur finally committed. Reproduced in the running app: 2 rows before the X, still 2 after it, 15 after clicking away. **Any blur-driven commit must `focus()` first when `doc.activeElement !== inp`.**
  - The mirror image, same function: a **debounced** commit can fire *after* focus has left (type, then click a checkbox inside the debounce window). The real blur already committed, so there is nothing to push - and blurring/refocusing there yanks the caret back into the search box a fifth of a second after the user left it. Bail out of the debounce when the field is no longer focused.

### State Management
- Widget values lost on step navigation - explicitly save to `persistent_*` session state keys before `st.rerun()`.
- Protect `on_click` array mutations against double-click: all mutations must be idempotent.
- After updating a UI placeholder before a heavy blocking operation, add `time.sleep(0.2)` to guarantee Streamlit flushes the DOM paint.

### Time remaining: ONE model, `engine/estimation.py`
- **Every phase estimates through `ProgressEstimator`.** It fits two channels - a **unit** cost (per file / recording / course / conversion) and a **byte** cost - by weighted least squares over a sliding window, **subject to conserving the window's wall-clock**. Read the module docstring before changing it; the maths is short but three of its details are non-obvious and each fixed a measured error:
  1. **Conservation is what makes it trustworthy.** In a steady run both channels advance together and are nearly collinear - "2.3 s/file" and "0.38 s/MB" fit identical data - so an unconstrained fit lands wherever its regulariser pulls and the ETA came out **2x** wrong with a clean residual. Constraining `unit·units + byte·MB = elapsed` over the window removes that freedom. It also prices in-window stalls for free.
  2. **Observations are binned (~2 s), never per event.** With 0.4 s repaints a bin that finishes one file has `dt = 0.4` no matter how long the file took, so a per-event fit read 0.25 s/file when the truth was 0.75.
  3. **Trailing dead time is excluded from the fit and credited against the projection instead.** Elapsed time accrues while no unit completes, so a 9 s/unit phase fitted anywhere from 9 s to 18 s depending on when you sampled it - ~50% high on average. Interior gaps stay in (a rate-limit sleep *between* completions really is part of a unit's cost).
- **Two presets, and the call site picks:** `transfer_estimator()` for byte-moving phases, `stepwise_estimator(prior_sec)` for phases that complete one slow unit at a time (analysis, conversions, transcription). The stepwise preset widens the window to minutes - the transfer defaults misread that rhythm. Priors are per-phase because a Word→PDF conversion and an HTML→MD rewrite differ by an order of magnitude.
- **"Estimating" now means only one thing: the caller has not said how much work there is.** Everything else produces a number, prefixed `~` while the model still leans on its priors. The old behaviour - no ETA at all until the first byte moved - was the actual bug report: a course that opens with 26 zero-byte shortcuts showed "Estimating" for 90 seconds and then printed 20:34, about 7x the truth.
- **Speed is windowed (10 s), not cumulative.** The cumulative average read 0.7 MB/s on a run moving several times faster, and the ETA divided by it.
- **Rates learn across phases**: `shared/helpers.py:learned_transfer_priors()` / `remember_transfer_priors()`. Session-scoped on purpose - they describe this network, not a preference.
- **A skipped file must leave the MB denominator.** `update_ui`'s `skipped` branch subtracts `file_size`. Without it, a re-run of an 849 MB course sat at `0.0 / 849.4 MB` from start to finish and the ETA priced in bytes that were never going to move. (The engine had always sent the size for exactly this; nothing consumed it.)
- **Dead time at either END of the window is removed; interior gaps stay.** They fail in opposite directions and both were measured. *Trailing* dead time is the unfinished unit (sawtooth: a 9 s/unit phase fitted anywhere from 9 s to 18 s). *Leading* dead time is the phase's one-off setup - 45 s of Canvas metadata latency before the first file - and charging it to the handful of files that arrive next made the first real estimate read **5:37:34** on a four-minute remainder. The leading run is TRIMMED, not dropped: it keeps as much lead-in as a typical unit gets in the rest of the window, which the window itself answers. Dropping it outright halved the fitted cost of stepwise phases.
- **Two brakes on extrapolation** (`_temper`): the byte cost is blended toward its prior in proportion to the MB actually observed, and capped at 2.5x the *measured* throughput. The cap also stops the two cells contradicting each other, which is what a user sees first ("3.3 MB/s, 828 MB left, why does it say five hours?"). The **unit** channel gets no blend and measurement says it must not - conservation already bounds it, and blending made a stepwise phase with a 2.8x-off prior settle at 112% error instead of 37%.
- **"Finishing…", not 00:00.** Canvas produces Pages / shortcuts / secondary content as it goes, raising numerator AND denominator together, so the last 30-50 files ran for half a minute behind 100% / total-of-total / 00:00. `is_open_ended` detects a total that grew *after* the counters already read done; the bar holds at 99% and the ETA says "Finishing…". Nothing knows how many are left - the point is to stop claiming otherwise. **The remainder guard needs `_OPEN_ENDED_SLACK_UNITS` (2), not zero**: the counters advance from different code paths a tick apart ("total grows to 201 / done catches up / total grows to 202"), so demanding an exactly-zero remainder made the cell alternate `Finishing…` / `00:01` about three times a second for the whole stretch - the screen that lied became a screen that strobed. `was_done` upstream is what keeps this narrow; a mid-run discovery with a real queue never sets the timestamp at all.
- `tests/test_estimation.py` scripts real run shapes and asserts accuracy; every regression there was a defect found while building it.

### The metrics row: one renderer, one set of cells
- `build_metrics_row(metrics)` in `engine/progress_dashboard.py` is the **only** place that markup exists. Compose from the named cells (`metric_transferred`, `metric_speed`, `metric_count`, `metric_eta`, `metric_elapsed`, `metric_value`) or, for any byte-moving phase, from `transfer_metrics(estimator, ...)` - which reads speed and ETA off the *same* estimator so the two can never disagree.
- The four hand-copied duplicates are gone. `Metric` carries `value` and `suffix` separately and **escapes both**, which is why no caller needs to hand-build markup any more.
- **Three depths, one instrument.** The metrics row and the active-file card are RAISED (translucent lift + outward shadow); the filename inside the active-file card and the terminal log below are RECESSED (dark well + inset shadow). The lift is a white overlay, not a palette colour, so one base style composes correctly inside the run card, inside Today's card and standalone - no per-host override.
- **Streamlit's `-16px` markdown margin eats card padding, INCONSISTENTLY.** Both completion cards carried a fat bottom (`35px`/`25px` against `20px`/`8px` elsewhere) that was silently paying for it. It hides because it depends on what the LAST child happens to be: a partial-success card ends in an expander and measures correctly, a plain success card ends in markdown and collapses to a third of its padding. Cancel the margin per card, then the declared padding is what lands. Corollary: once cancelled, a *sibling* container's `margin-top` gains back 16px - the Panopto summary needs `-4px` to land 12px on screen. Measure the rendered gap; never trust the number you wrote.
- **Analysis renders through the same chrome**: `render_analysis_dashboard()` gives the scan phase the same header / bar / metrics row as the download, in the same card, from all three call sites (download scan, sync analysis, the sync two-pass shell). It previously drew its own heading + 8px hairline and carried no metrics at all, so the first screen of a run looked like a different application than the one ten seconds later.
- **The bar and the row must measure the SAME unit** - `analysis_percent(courses_done, courses_total, sub_done, sub_total)`. Both analysis call sites used to pass the *sub-step's* ratio straight to the bar while the row counted courses, so every time a module list finished the card showed a **100% bar above "COURSES 0 / 2"**. Whole courses plus the current one's fraction is also monotonic across the whole scan instead of resetting per course.
- **Nothing a counter can hold may raise or render as a non-number.** These builders run inside repaint loops, several of them *not* wrapped in try/except, so a cell that raises takes the run's progress UI with it - and the values arrive from a dozen counters owned by different subsystems. Every cell coerces through `_num` (None / NaN / inf / non-numeric → 0), and:
  - `build_progress_bar_html` clamps through `_pct` to **0-100**. This is not padding: the value goes into `width: N%`, and `-3` / `nan` are *invalid CSS*, so the declaration is dropped and a block div falls back to the full track - "less than nothing happened" renders identically to "finished". `sync/analysis.py` was passing an unclamped `int(current / total * 100)`.
  - `build_metrics_row([])` returns `''`; the surface with no cells in it reads as a panel that failed to load.
  - `metric_count` drops a **zero** denominator for the same reason `metric_transferred` drops "/ 0.0 MB", and floors the denominator at the numerator ("236 / 233").
  - `tests/test_progress_dashboard.py` guards all of it. Every case there was found by probing the real builders.

### A keyed card can be INHERITED by the next element (Critical)
- Streamlit reconciles by **position**. If a phase change shifts the elements after a `st.container(key=...)` by one slot, the container's DOM node - class list and all - is handed to whatever element inherits that index. On the download screen the inheritor was the empty post-processing cancel placeholder, which rendered as a **stray 38px empty card** below the Cancel button that no Python code was drawing. Same family as the dialog-ordering bug above.
- **A section whose SHAPE depends on state must render inside one always-present container.** Same failure, second instance (2026-07-27): the transcription dialog's GPU-enablement section emitted 1 element idle and 3 while provisioning, so clicking "Download CUDA libraries" pushed everything below down two slots - the advisory container landed on the model table's index, inherited its children, and drew a **second, stale copy of the whole model table** above the real one, fading grey/active twice a second with the progress rerun. The fix is `st.container(key="pan_gpu_fix_slot")` wrapping every branch (the same trick `pan_advisory_slot` already used one element lower), plus rendering the running state inside the SAME keyed `pan_cuda_card` as the idle state so the block shape matches at both levels. Verified by recording `(rows/tables/headers)` per animation frame across a start→download→cancel cycle: **one** signature, `6/1/1`.
- **Fix the indices, not the ghost.** Scanning and downloading now build ONE `progress_dashboard` card between them (`if download_status in ('running', 'scanning')`), with a single Cancel button whose *label* changes per phase. `global.css` carries a `:not(:has([data-testid="stMarkdownContainer"]))` net for the same reason a seatbelt exists - the failure is silent and invisible in review - but the net is not the fix.
- The `else` branch's `status_text` / `progress_container` / `mb_counter` / `log_area` in `app.py` are dead (nothing reads them) and cost nothing - a bare `st.empty()` renders a **zero-height** `stElementContainer` (measured 2026-07-27: a completion screen is byte-identical with and without one). They are load-bearing for a different reason: they are the slots that tear the run dashboard's node down. Do not delete them.

### A container inherits the previous run's CHILDREN, not just its node (Critical)
- Same index, one level deeper: `AppRoot.addBlock` **reuses the children** of whatever block already sits at that delta path, when the block `type` matches (read out of the 1.51 bundle): `if (existing instanceof BlockNode && existing.deltaBlock.type === block.type) children = existing.children`. A run dashboard and a completion card are both `vertical`, so a completion card landing on `progress_dashboard`'s index is handed its five children - the **metrics row and terminal log render inside the green "Download Success" card**. They survive until `clearStaleNodes`, which runs only when the script run **finishes**, so the slower the completion branch the longer the broken frame; then the card shrinks and everything below it jumps up. Measured pre-fix on the real sync completion screen: **537px mid-run → 157px after**.
- **The obvious fix is silently useless.** `slot = st.empty(); slot.container(…)` puts both deltas on the SAME delta path (a placeholder hands its locked cursor to `_block`), and `ForwardMsgQueue.enqueue` **composes two deltas at one path into the last one** - the empty never reaches the browser and the children are inherited exactly as before. Streamlit's own comment there calls this out as the trick `st.write` relies on. **The empty must land on a DIFFERENT index**: two separate top-level calls. That is `shared/components.py:fresh_container`, which every terminal screen's card goes through.
- `sync/completion.py` emits **two** leading empties, because `run_sync` has one CONDITIONAL element before its dashboard (the macOS `_tcc_batch_active` notice) - the dashboard sits on the first empty without it and the second with it.
- **The other half of the same symptom is blocking I/O.** `CanvasManager.__init__` does a `requests.get` to resolve vanity URLs, and the download completion screen built one manager **per course, twice** (both loops only wanted the pure `_sanitize_filename`). That is the ~1s the screen spent streaming itself in card by card. Resolution is now memoised per URL and the screen constructs one manager. A terminal screen must do no blocking I/O - it is supposed to appear in one frame.
- **The artifact is only VISIBLE in proportion to the work the run does AFTER the swap** - which is why the sweep found the sync page guilty and the others innocent (measured 2026-07-31). `clearStaleNodes` prunes the leftovers when the **script run finishes**, so the window is "time from the swap to the end of the run". The sync page spends ~400ms after its list, so leftovers were plainly visible. The Saved Groups hub has the same shape (`hub_pair_card_{i}` ↔ `hub_compact_edit_form_{i}`, 3 children vs 4 when the folder exists) and is **clean in practice**, because a dialog body is a fragment whose run ends in ~20ms. Today's Manage/Done is clean because both arms have the same child count. Do not "fix" the hub on the strength of the pattern alone - measure the transition first; a padding change there would be churn with no user-visible effect.
- **In a LIST, `fresh_container` is the wrong instrument - make the replacement ISOMORPHIC instead** (measured 2026-07-30, the sync page's inline add/edit pair form). When a row of a list is swapped for a different widget, there is no "clean index" to move to: every index in the list holds another row of the same shape, so a leading `st.empty()` just shifts the form onto the NEXT row and it inherits that one instead. **Both directions count**: the form also replaces the "Add Course" row, so that row is wrapped in `st.container(key="sync_add_row_slot")` and padded to five children too - without it, cancelling out of the form left the form's "Cancel" and "Confirm and Add" buttons under the restored row for 22ms (slot 112px instead of 48px). `shared/components.py:pad_slot_children` is the one helper; `SYNC_LIST_SLOT_CHILDREN` is the one number. A padded container MUST set `gap: 0` - four zero-height children still cost four gaps. The form must match the row it replaces on **both** counts:
  - **Slots** - `ui/sync_dialogs.py:render_pending_folder_ui` emits exactly ONE top-level element, like the row's single `st.columns(...)`. With two (it briefly used `fresh_container`) every item below shifted by one and the tail of the old render stayed on screen: a **duplicated "Add Course" row for ~25ms** when cancelling out of the form.
  - **Children** - the row is `st.columns([5, 1.5, 1.1, 1.5, 1.2])`, i.e. **five** children, and the form had four. The inherited fifth - the row's red **Remove** button - was never overwritten and sat *inside the open edit form* until `clearStaleNodes` (form **247px instead of 193px**). The form now ends with a bare `st.empty()` purely to be the fifth child. Change the row's column count and that padding must change with it; `tests/test_event_container_clobber.py` asserts both numbers.
  - Reported as "the old grey course-card shifts to just below the edit form and then disappears", and "clicking Add Course shows an empty box with only the Add Course button in it, then the form loads in above it". Verified after: Edit, Cancel and Add Course each move the list through **exactly one state**, no intermediate frame.

### Verify in the REAL app, not a mock (Critical)
- An isolated Streamlit harness proves how *Streamlit* behaves. It does **not** prove a change works here. Build one to answer a narrow DOM question, then run `streamlit run app.py` and drive the actual screen with Playwright before calling anything done.
- **1,431 passing unit tests cannot see an `UnboundLocalError`, and one real download can.** Worked example, 2026-07-29: the flat-mode dispatch in `download_course_async` was given `isolate_pages=isolate` — but `isolate` is a local of `get_course_files_metadata`, a *different* function, and the only same-idea name in scope (`_iso`) is bound inside **both** an `if debug_mode:` and an `if secondary_content_settings:`. The engine catches the exception and reports it as a generic "Processing Error", so the whole course downloaded **nothing** and the only clue on screen was `cannot access local variable 'isolate'`. Every structural test still passed, because they assert *source shape* and the shape was right. The fix binds `_isolate_secondary` unconditionally next to the other per-course state; `tests/test_page_isolation_flat_mode.py` walks the AST to prove it is not nested inside an `if`/`try`.
- **A brittle test anchor reads like a missing guard.** The same change broke `test_canvas_content_runs_before_every_files_tab_sweep`, which matched `"if mode == 'flat':\n                    downloaded_files_info"` as one exact string — inserting a comment between those two lines failed it with `substring not found`, which looks like the ordering guard is gone rather than like the source was reformatted. Anchor an ordering test on the two statements and compare positions; never on them being adjacent.
- Worked example, 2026-07-26: the run-dashboard card looked perfect in a mock that called `engine.progress_dashboard.render_metrics_row`. The **sync flow did not call it** - it used `build_metrics_html` in the same module, a byte-identical fourth copy of the markup. The new `cd-metrics-row` hook landed on three copies and missed the only one the sync screen renders, so the real app still showed a box inside a box. **Those four copies are now gone** - see "The metrics row" below - but the lesson stands: find the call site the screen actually uses before believing a harness.
- The reachable-state problem is solved: `scripts/seed_today_test.py "<folder>" [--new N --updates M]` primes a synced course folder so a sync produces a known set of New / Clean Update rows, which is what gets you to the review, confirm, progress and completion screens. It deletes files with no backup, so it needs the user's say-so - but the app does **not** need to be closed.

### A swallowed exception in a repaint hook hides the whole panel
- `sync/analysis.py:sync_progress_hook` wraps its body in `except Exception` so a failed repaint can never abort an analysis. That is correct - but it must **log**. `analyzing_heading_html` was imported only inside the Today-mode branch, so the regular full-page branch raised `NameError` on *every* tick and the analysis panel silently never painted: users saw a bare "Cancel Sync" button on an empty page until the review screen appeared. Months of that, invisible, because nothing was written anywhere.
- Rule: a bare `except Exception: pass` around UI code is a bug waiting to be invisible. Log at `warning` with `exc_info=True`.

### A global `summary::before` chevron becomes a third flex item
- `global.css` draws the app's chevron on every `<summary>`. A summary that is a `justify-content: space-between` flex row of exactly **two** children (icon+label | value) gains a **third** child, which takes the left slot and shoves the real content into the middle of the row. That is what put the Confirm Sync dialog's "Files:" row visibly out of line with the static "Total Size:" / "Destination:" rows below it.
- Any such row must be added to the opt-out list next to `.stat-row-dropdown > details > summary::before` and carry its own chevron inside the row (see `.arrow-icon` in `ui/sync_confirmation.py` - a masked lucide glyph, `rotate(90deg)` on `details[open]`, placed *before* the value).

### An in-flow leading icon steals a button's centring
- A `::before` icon on a button is a flex sibling of the label, so the browser centres `(icon + gap + label)` as one block and the **text** ends up off-centre. Measured on Confirm Sync: "No, Go back" sat 15px right of its button's centre while "Yes, Start Sync" was dead centre - side by side that reads as broken alignment.
- Fix: `position: absolute; left: <inset>; top: 50%; transform: translateY(-50%)` on the `::before` (and `position: relative` on the button). The label then centres on the same axis as a plain button's.

### Disabled buttons: ONE recipe, and nothing on top of it
- `styles/global.css` `button[disabled] { filter: brightness(0.5) saturate(0.5) }` is the **only** disabled paint in the app. Two JS gates mirror it verbatim (`shared/components.py:live_enable_button`, `ui/course_selector.py:_gate_actions_on_selection`) because they grey a genuinely-*enabled* button.
- **Three things must never be added on top**, and a 2026-07-26 sweep removed 20 sites that did:
  1. **`opacity`** - it MULTIPLIES with the filter. The sync-history Open/Reveal icon buttons had `opacity: 0.3` on top (0.5 × 0.3 = 15% of their enabled paint - effectively invisible). Same for the sidebar nav (0.4), logout (0.35), model Download (0.45), the GPU pill (0.38, three stacked rules).
  2. **A second `filter`** - `filter` is ONE property, so a local `filter: grayscale(100%)` with higher specificity *replaces* the shared one and the button reverts to exactly the flat grey slab the recipe exists to avoid (`btn_pan_layout_`, `btn_sec_org_`, `btn_pan_out_txt/srt`, `_retry_failed_btn`, `save_pair_`).
  3. **A flat background/colour repaint** - `background: #3a3a3a; color: #6b6b6b`. Because the two primary sync buttons have *different* enabled colours, repainting both to the same grey made them look like two unrelated controls only when disabled. `sync_hub.css` had the broadest offender: `div[data-testid="stDialog"] button[kind="primary"][disabled]` repainted every disabled primary button in every dialog.
- **The one sanctioned exemption is a "rerun lock"** - the invisible full-card hit targets behind the Card 2/3/4 headers, which are `disabled=True` only for the duration of a rerun. That button IS the card's visible surface, so they opt out with an explicit `filter: none` (plus a comment saying why).
- `opacity: 1` does NOT undo the shared filter. The sidebar's "you are here, and it's running" nav button used only that and was dimmed anyway for months; it now uses `filter: none`.

### The step tracker: string ids, native buttons, one static stylesheet
- **Steps are identified by a STRING id, never a position** (`shared/helpers.py:SYNC_WIZARD_STEPS` / `DOWNLOAD_WIZARD_STEPS`). The integer contract shipped two lies at once: the sync flow rendered the **analysis phase and the review screen both as "step 2"**, so the whole scan ran under the label "Review Changes"; and Quick Sync — which jumps straight from analysis to sync (`sync/analysis.py`) — advertised a Review step it structurally never visits. Ids also let a step be **inserted** (the download flow's scan phase, previously hidden inside "Downloading") without renumbering eleven call sites.
- **Sync**: `select · analyze · review · sync · complete` ("Download & Sync"). **Download**: `select · configure · analyze · download · complete` ("Configure Download"). Display numbers are position + 1.
- **Quick Sync SKIPS review, it does not remove it** — struck through, `(skipped)`, dimmer than idle (idle means "not yet", skipped means "not at all"). Both modes therefore share one skeleton and one child count, which is load-bearing (see the index note below). On step 1 the mode is not chosen yet, so **nothing** is skipped: the tracker states what the flow CAN do before it states what this run will do.
- **It is a row of NATIVE `st.button`s, and it must stay that way.** Users try to click the previous step; it was one `st.html` block, i.e. a shadow root nothing outside could hear. The tempting fix — keep the HTML, have a `components.html` bridge click a hidden button — is a mechanism this repo has **already removed once** for being silently unreliable (`sync_ui.py`'s two-pass comment): its iframe realm dies every rerun and a dead bridge fails without a trace. A real button needs no bridge and is keyboard-reachable.
- **Only a step the run has already PASSED and whose page passed a handler is clickable** (`nav={'select': …}`), wired as `on_click` — never `if st.button(): …; st.rerun()`, which renders twice and drops the scroll anchor. Three pages opt in, each reusing the **same function its own Go-back button calls** (`_dl_settings_go_back`, `_quick_download_go_back`, `_sync_review_go_back`), so a tracker click can never reach a state the button could not — the `came_from_quick_dl` hand-back and `cleanup_sync_state()` included. Running phases are left via Cancel, never via the tracker.
- **Every step but one is `disabled` by design**, so the tracker carries an explicit `filter: none` exemption from the app's one disabled recipe. Same call as the card-header rerun locks: these are information, not controls the user failed to unlock.
- **The stylesheet is STATIC and lives in global.css**, selected off the widget key `cd_wiz_<flow>_<idx>_<step id>_st_<state>_<separator>`. This is not a style preference: the tracker is the **first element on six screens**, and an `st.markdown(<style>)` above it is a flex item (the ghost-box purge rule is inert), so one injection would push every one of those screens down a whole gap slot. Glyphs are `mask-image` + `background-color: currentColor`, which is what collapses 7 ids × 4 states into 7 rules.
- **Measured in 1.51, both non-obvious:** a button label is markdown, so a bare `1. Select Courses` is an **ordered-list item** and Streamlit eats the number (`1\.` is the escape); and a plain-text label renders with **no `<p>`** — the text sits directly in `stMarkdownContainer`, so `button p` matches nothing. A label containing inline markup (the skipped step's `~~…~~`) *does* get a `<p>`. Target `button [data-testid="stMarkdownContainer"]`, which covers both.
- **A screen that renders the tracker must render it in EVERY phase it can be in.** The sync analysis pass-1 shell and the `pre_sync` screen had no tracker while pass 2 and `syncing` did — which, now that the tracker is a `vertical` block, would put `st.container(key="progress_dashboard")` on index 0 in one frame and index 1 in the next, and `addBlock` hands a block the **children** of whatever occupied its index (see "inherits the dashboard's CHILDREN"). The tracker would render the header, bar, metrics and active-file rows inside itself. Both now render it.
- Covered by `tests/test_wizard_steps.py`, including a check that every step id has a glyph rule in global.css — a missing one is a silent, CSS-only empty box.

### The run dashboard card: the LAST row carries no margin
- The card sets `gap: 0` and an even 18px inset, so each row buys its separation from the row below with its own `margin-bottom`. That is right until the row **is** the last one, where the margin lands on top of the card's padding. The analysis dashboard has no terminal log, so its active-file card was last and sat **12 + 18 = 30px** off the bottom against 18px on the sides — the reported "unharmonious padding". `engine/progress_dashboard.py:_row_gap(last)` is the one place that decides; `render_analysis_dashboard` works out which row is last (metrics when there is no active file) rather than the card having to know which phase is running. Measured after the fix on the real sync analysis screen: **19px top / 19px bottom**.

### Sticky action bar
- Native `position: sticky` on the `stLayoutWrapper` (never the keyed div), one CSS block in `global.css`. Two opt-in paths: `st.container(key="sticky_actions_<page>")`, or a keyed selector matching the row (`action_dl_back`, `btn_sync_selected`) when the `with col:` blocks also hold click handlers.
- **Full bleed needs THREE declarations, not one.** `margin-inline: calc(-1 * var(--cd-page-pad))` alone only *slides* the bar to the page edge - Streamlit's `width: 100%` resolves against the containing block's CONTENT box and ignores margins. Adding `width: calc(100% + 2 * var(--cd-page-pad))` is then silently clamped by Streamlit's `max-width: 100%` on the same element (measured: even an inline `width: 1007px` reported 847px), so `max-width` must be restated too.
- **`--cd-page-pad` (declared in `global.css`) is the page's side padding, and we own it.** It mirrors Streamlit's own `applyWideModePadding()`: `spacing.lg` (1rem), swapped for `wideSidePadding` (5rem) above `contentMaxWidth 46rem + 2*(5rem - 1rem) = 54rem`. We also *apply* it to `stMainBlockContainer`, so the bleed can never drift from the padding.
- **A UI zoom/scale setting was built and REVERTED on 2026-07-26 - do not rebuild it.** CSS `zoom` on `:root` measured clean in Chromium (no overflow, shell exactly the viewport, dialogs anchored) and still broke the app in practice: the sticky bars detached and fell back to their static position, and slider thumbs stopped tracking the cursor by a couple of centimetres because Streamlit's widgets do their own JS hit-testing in unzoomed coordinates. Making it work would mean auditing every JS-measured widget, which is not worth it for a preference.
- **No border and no shadow at rest** - the surface + padding alone separate the bar. A separator hairline appears ONLY while the bar is genuinely pinned, via `body[data-cd-stuck="1"]`, set by `shared/components.py:inject_app_shell_bridge()` (an IntersectionObserver with `threshold: 1` + `rootMargin: 0 0 -1px 0` against `stMain`).
- **The flag lives on `<body>`, not on the bar (changed 2026-07-26).** Streamlit *replaces* the bar's wrapper element on every rerun, so an attribute set there was gone for ~50 measured milliseconds afterwards and the separator blinked off and back on for every full-page rerun. `<body>` is never replaced, so a freshly-rendered bar is already correct on its first frame. **Same rule, same reason, for `ui/course_selector.py:_gate_actions_on_selection`** - its `data-cd-has-sel` used to sit on the buttons, so a new button carried no attribute and both primary actions flashed at *full brightness* before dropping back to the disabled paint. Its CSS is written `body:not([data-cd-has-sel="1"])` so unknown state reads as unavailable, never as available. **Any JS-driven paint on a Streamlit-owned element has this bug**; put the state on `<body>` and select downward. It is an `inset` box-shadow, not a `border-top`, so appearing/disappearing never shifts the row by a pixel. Deleting that one rule removes the line in every state; nothing else reads the attribute. CSS `@container scroll-state(stuck:)` would do this natively but is Chromium-only; macOS renders in WKWebView.
- **The bar normalises its content's margins** (`margin-top/bottom: 0` on the row it wraps). Custom Download pulls its action row up by `-15px`, which used to eat the bar's 14px top padding and leave the separator sitting on the buttons' own border. Measured after the fix: 14.9px from divider to button on *both* opt-in paths.
- **Not every page qualifies.** Sync step 1's action row has the Sync History section *below* it, so a `bottom: 0` sticky element would stay pinned over the history for the rest of the scroll. Confirm Sync is an `@st.dialog` (own scroll). The completion screens' single "Go to front page" is a leave-the-screen action, not a CTA you scroll to reach.

### Help text: one gate, and where the line is
- **The Help card is a PURE-CSS toggle, not a rerun** (`shared/components.py:render_help_card`): a hidden checkbox plus `label for=` triggers. A `label` activates its control from anywhere in the document, so the trigger can sit in the page header while the checkbox lives with the card below - only the checkbox and the card need a shared parent, for `~`. Do **not** put it back on `st.button` + `st.rerun()`: re-rendering the whole page to show a static explainer is what produced all three reported glitches (list flash, buttons flashing bright, captions fading) - see the conditional-`<style>` and `<body>`-attribute rules above. Verified in a harness first: Streamlit keeps raw `<input>` markup, a `label for=` still activates a checkbox inside a `display: none` ancestor, and the checked state **survives an unrelated rerun** because React leaves an unchanged `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` alone - so no JS bridge is needed to persist it.
  - A closed card must cost nothing, so its element-container is collapsed with `:has(.cd-help-cb:not(:checked)) { display: none }` - a zero-*height* flex item would still eat one `gap` slot on all six screens. Closed is the CSS default, so a `:has()` that fails to match leaves the card hidden, not shown.
- `shared/helpers.py:help_text_enabled()` backs Settings → **Show help text** (default ON). It gates exactly one category: **copy that explains the UI and occupies permanent screen space** — `render_help_card` (all 8 call sites route through it, so one early-return covers them), the `.cd-action-hint` captions under the primary actions, and the sync page's intro line.
- It deliberately does **not** gate: anything operational (errors, warnings, empty states, counts); the reason a control is unavailable (hiding that turns a disabled button into a dead end); option labels and card descriptions in the download settings (that's how you know what a toggle does); or `help=` tooltips (they cost no space until asked for).
- **The test that decides it:** would a power user who already knows the app still need this on screen? If no, it's help text.

### Primary-action captions, not tooltips (`.cd-action-hint`)
- The four primary actions (Custom/Quick Download, Analyze/Quick Sync) each carry a one-line centred caption underneath. They used to be `help=`/`title` tooltips; that was **wrong** — a tooltip fires every single time you move the pointer to click, so a returning user reads the app as nagging. The tooltip on those buttons now carries **only** the reason it is unavailable.
- **They render AFTER the sticky bar, never inside it** (`st.container(key="action_hints_courses")`, same column widths). Inside, they were faded to `opacity: 0` while the bar floated — which still **reserved their height in the pinned bar**, lifting both buttons 41 measured pixels and covering a course row for no benefit. `display: none` was not available as a fix *there*: removing them from the bar's flow changes its height, which moves the point at which sticky releases, which flips `data-cd-stuck`, which puts them back — a two-state oscillation at the release threshold. Rendering them outside dissolves the trade instead of picking a side: the bar's height no longer depends on them at all, so a pinned bar is **exactly** as tall as it is with help text off, and the captions simply sit below the fold while pinned and scroll into view under their own buttons as it releases.
- The caption row gives back Streamlit's 1rem block gap **and** its own 7px (`margin-top: -1rem` on the row, `margin-top: 0` on the hint) so it lands flush with the bar's bottom edge — 14px under the button, one bar-padding. Do not pull it any higher: the bar is opaque and `z-index: 20`, so anything overlapping it is simply hidden.
- Their columns use `vertical_alignment="top"` so both captions start on the same line when one wraps; the `OR` separator gets `.cd-action-or` (`min-height: 3.2em`, flex-centred) to stay level with the buttons instead of the column.

### Don't fade the page behind a modal — and the delay is an ANIMATION, not a transition
- Streamlit marks the previous run's elements `data-stale="true"` and applies `opacity: .33` with `transition: opacity 1s ease-in 0.5s` (read out of the 1.51 bundle). Two rules in `global.css` tame it: **no fade at all while a dialog is open** (the background is inert and unclickable, so the signal is pure noise), and **a 2.5s delay globally** so a dialog *handoff* — where the old modal is gone and the new one hasn't rendered — never crosses the threshold either.
- That handoff window is why the flash was **asymmetric**: Settings → Panopto warms a hardware probe first and took long enough to fade; Panopto → Settings was instant and didn't. Anything in this app that legitimately takes longer than 2.5s already has purpose-built progress UI.
- **The delay is `animation: cd-stale-fade 1s ease-in 2.5s both !important`, and it must not be turned back into `transition-delay` (changed 2026-07-27).** A transition was the wrong instrument in two ways, both invisible in review and both found by sampling computed styles per frame in the running app:
  1. **A component can silently disarm it.** A delay only delays a *transition*, and an element only transitions the properties in its own `transition-property`. `_render_multi_select_list` declares `transition: background-color 0.2s !important` on every course row (the hover/checked tint), which drops `opacity` out of that list — so the stale fade stopped being a transition at all and became an **instant** jump to .33 that no delay could reach. Measured: course row `opacity 0.33 / transition-property background-color`, a plain button beside it `opacity 1 / transition-property all`. That dip across 43 elements is the reported "the course list dips to black when I toggle a checkbox — it didn't use to".
  2. **A transition can never run on a newly inserted element** — there is no previous value to animate from, so the first computed style is painted as-is. Search-as-you-type re-creates the course rows on every keystroke, so a row arriving already stale painted .33 immediately. Fixing (1) alone still left dip frames for exactly this reason.
  - An animation fixes both: it runs from the moment the element renders, and `both` fill-mode applies the `from` keyframe *during* the 2.5s delay, so a fresh stale node computes to opacity 1. The long-operation signal is unchanged (verified 1 → 1 → 1 → .94 → .75 → .33 sampled across 3.6s).
  - **`!important` goes on `animation`, never on `opacity`** — an `!important` opacity outranks an animation and would freeze the fade off entirely. That same rule is *why* the dialog-open override still wins: its `opacity: 1 !important` beats the animation by spec.
  - **The rule needs its own `div[data-testid="stDialog"]` selector.** `st.dialog` renders into a portal, *not* inside `stMain`, so a suppression scoped to stMain/stSidebar misses every in-dialog list — which is why the main course selector went flash-free while the "Select Course to sync" and "Saved Groups & Pairs → Change Course" lists kept flashing. Measured there before the selector existed: `opacity 0.33 / transition-property background-color / transition-delay 0s / animation-name none` — the identical instant snap, because `_render_single_select_list` injects the same `transition: background-color 0.2s !important` as its multi-select twin. A dialog checkbox click is also a **full-page** rerun (a dialog is not a fragment), so it is not even a fast one. This does not undo the "no fade behind an open modal" rule (still stMain/stSidebar-scoped, so a dialog element going stale keeps its own feedback) — a dialog operation slower than 2.5s still fades; only the sub-second strobe is gone. Verified after: click-row / per-letter typing / Favorites-All pill = 1,275 frames, **0** dips, dialog height a single 905px value throughout.

### Buttons as Cards ("Native Button is the Card")
- Never overlay invisible buttons over `st.markdown` content - Streamlit's React DOM will collapse the hitbox.
- Style native `st.button` as a card using CSS (`height`, `background-image`, `::before`/`::after` pseudo-elements).
- Icons → Base64 via `get_base64_image()`, injected into CSS `background-image`.
- Use `::before` for icon layers in buttons next to text.

### HTML Safety
- All Canvas data interpolated into `st.html()` or `st.markdown(unsafe_allow_html=True)` must be wrapped with `esc()` (`html.escape`) to prevent XSS/DOM corruption.
- **Escape EVERY piece of a split value, not just the one that looks like text.** A Canvas filename split by `os.path.splitext` is two halves of the same untrusted string. `ui/sync_review.py` escaped `esc(_name)` and interpolated the extension raw on the **same line** for months: a file called `notes.<img src=x onerror=…>` put a live tag on the review screen. Found 2026-07-27 by the architecture audit, once Rule 4 stopped drowning in false positives.
- **`.upper()` / `.lower()` are not sanitisers.** HTML tag and attribute names are case-insensitive, so `"<img src=x onerror=y>".upper()` is still a working tag. A case transform can only *preserve* safety, never create it - which is exactly why `_SAFE_STR_METHODS` in the audit script accepts them only on an already-safe receiver.
- **`scripts/verify_architecture.py` Rule 4 is a real gate again** (0 unsuppressed violations, `tests/test_architecture_audit.py` guards both directions). It tracks assignment provenance, so `x = esc(v)` then `{x}` is understood; it is per-function and uses a pessimistic fixpoint. Treat a new hit as a real finding rather than reaching for `# audit-ignore`.

## Startup: the window is NEVER empty
- **The splash is painted TWICE, by two different documents, and they must be identical.** `start.py` shows it in the pywebview window; `scripts/patch_streamlit_boot.py` injects the same markup into Streamlit's bundled `index.html` at build time, immediately after `<body>` so it is the new document's first paint. The hand-off at `load_url()` is then invisible. Colours, sizes and the label live in both files - `tests/test_startup.py` asserts they still match, because a difference there IS the flicker.
  - The label is `16.8px` with `line-height: normal` on **both** sides, not `1.05rem`: the overlay lives in a document whose stylesheet we do not own, so a rem would follow Streamlit's root size and an inherited line-height would change the label's box and shift the spinner at the swap.
- **Why it cannot be done any other way**: `/_stcore/health` answers as soon as tornado *binds*, and that is the only readiness signal the launcher has - the frontend, the websocket session and the first script run all come after it. Measured 2026-07-27 in the real app: `load_url()` → **+2.9s** React mounts → **+6.9s** first content. Streamlit's `index.html` carries no background and an empty `#root`, so all of that is a dark window. There is no supported custom-index hook; pywebview cannot inject a script before document creation; wrapping the app in an iframe of our own page would change the document topology every `window.parent` bridge, the `stDialog` portal and the native dialogs depend on. Patching one static file at build time is the smallest change that guarantees byte-0 continuity, and it is the same mechanism `patch_streamlit_webkit.py` already uses.
- **The overlay hides on REAL readiness, and can never trap the user.** It waits for content in `stMain` (an `stElementContainer` taller than 8px - style-only injections are 0-height and cannot be mistaken for a page) plus `window.prerenderReady`, which Streamlit sets when the first script run finishes and which survives `st.stop()` and any `st.rerun()` chain. Two escape hatches, both load-bearing: **5s after content first appears** it reveals regardless (a launch that goes straight into the daily auto-sync keeps the script RUNNING for minutes, so `prerenderReady` never arrives and the app's own live progress UI is the right thing to show), and a **30s absolute cap**. `finish()` is also armed on a timer next to its double-`requestAnimationFrame`, because rAF does not fire while the window is occluded.
- **Nothing may be emitted into `index.html` containing `{{`, `{%` or `{#`.** Tornado serves it through its TEMPLATE engine on the 404 path (`routes.py:write_error`), so a stray opener takes the page down on any unknown URL. The patch script asserts this.
- **Session restore runs during INIT, not from the login page** (`ui.auth.restore_saved_session`, called from `app.py` between `ensure_download_state()` and `_write_nav_to_query_params()`). It used to sit inside `render_login_page` and end in `st.rerun()`, so every launch with a saved login spent an entire script run doing keyring + Canvas I/O behind an empty window and then threw it away. Two silent consequences of the old placement are also gone: config settings were adopted *after* the sidebar had rendered, and the once-per-session stale-debug-log clear could never fire because `debug_mode` had not been read yet.
- **A URL that is already `*.instructure.com` is never "resolved"** (`core.canvas_logic._is_canonical_canvas_host`). Vanity resolution exists to follow a custom domain to its instructure.com target, so pointing it at the target fetches the whole Canvas landing page to learn nothing - 0.70s measured, on the startup path, before a single pixel existed. The check matches the HOST, so `foo.instructure.com.example.net` is still a vanity domain like any other.
- **`_find_free_port` must not set `SO_REUSEADDR` on Windows.** There it does not mean "reuse a TIME_WAIT port" as it does on Unix - it means "bind even if another process is LISTENING", so the probe handed out occupied ports. Measured twice on 2026-07-27: with a server already on 8501 the launcher picked 8501, Streamlit bound it a second time, and the health check was answered by the *other* process. Tornado skips the flag on Windows for the same reason.
- **Both prewarms in `start.py` are pure overlap, never a behaviour change.** `_prewarm_app_modules` imports the app's graph on a daemon thread while the server boots (safe concurrently: zero module-level import cycles across the 68 local modules, and `app.py` - the only file with module-level `st.*` calls - is never imported); `_prewarm_frontend_assets` pulls the 23 MB static bundle into the page cache, entry bundle first, frozen builds only. Frozen builds also pass `--server.fileWatcherType=none`: a packaged app's sources cannot change, and the watcher installs an observer per local module and re-walks `sys.modules` after every rerun.

## The macOS Keychain prompt must never be on the SCRIPT THREAD (2026-08-21)
`restore_saved_session()` runs during init, on the Streamlit script thread, before a single element renders - and `keyring`'s macOS backend calls `SecItemCopyMatching` with **no UI-suppression flag**. When the saved item's ACL does not trust the running binary that call **BLOCKS on a system prompt**, so the script never finishes and the frontend has nothing to render. Measured in the PACKAGED app on macOS 26.6.1, frames captured:

| t | what the user saw |
|---|---|
| 0-30 s | boot overlay, "Connecting…" + spinner |
| **30-90 s** | the overlay's absolute cap fires and the window is **COMPLETELY EMPTY** |
| Deny, or the 90 s watchdog | the login page, **with no explanation at all** |

Nothing on screen connected the empty window to the Keychain dialog, and `_load_fallback_token` is a deliberate no-op off Windows - so a denial silently means "go and find a Canvas token again".

- **It fires on every app UPDATE, not once.** The bundle is ad-hoc signed (`TeamIdentifier=not set`), and **both** halves of a keychain item's access control key on the code signature: the trusted-application list *and*, since 10.11, the **partition list**.
- **TWO ESCAPES ARE MEASURED AND CLOSED - do not re-derive them.** `security add-generic-password -A` (trusted-app list `<null>`) **still prompts**: the ACL dump shows `partition_id: apple-tool:`, and the partition list gates access independently. And the app **cannot silently repair its own ACL** afterwards - the delete half of delete+add needs authorisation we do not have and fails **-25244**. That failure is SAFE (the item survives), which is why the attempt is UI-suppressed or not made at all. So while the app is ad-hoc signed the prompt is **unavoidable**; only its cost is fixable.
- **`Allow` and `Always Allow` are not interchangeable, and that is the single most valuable thing the notice says.** Measured by re-reading with UI suppressed after each click: **`Allow` leaves the ACL UNCHANGED**, so the prompt returns on **every launch, for ever**; **`Always Allow` updates it** and the prompt does not come back until the next update. A student who picks the safer-sounding button gets the worse outcome permanently, and nothing else in the product would ever tell them.
- **The fix is a NON-INTERACTIVE probe, and it is a structural guarantee rather than a tuned timeout.** `keyring_get_without_prompting()` flips `SecKeychainSetUserInteractionAllowed(false)` around the read, so "macOS would put a modal in front of the user" stops being an unbounded block and becomes an error we see in milliseconds - **4.3 ms** on success, **9.6 ms** when a prompt would be needed. `needs_prompt` means *exactly* that: a missing item, or any other failure, answers `(None, False)`, because prompting cannot fix those.
  - Suppression is **PROCESS-GLOBAL**, so the window is held under `_keychain_ui_lock` and restored in a `finally`. A probe that finds the lock already taken **returns `(None, True)` rather than waiting** - waiting for the in-flight unlock is the exact blocking this function exists to remove.
  - **The seam is platform-independent on purpose.** `restore_saved_session` calls `keyring_get_without_prompting` on every platform (Windows delegates straight to the old watchdogged read). Had the darwin branch been the only caller, a regression would **pass on Windows and fail only on a Mac** - which is precisely how this class of bug survives in this repo.
- **The prompt is raised as the LAST statement of `render_login_page`, and the position IS the fix.** The script run ends on the next line, so the explanation has already been emitted and the window paints before the system dialog can land on top of it. Starting it any earlier risks the dialog appearing over a window that is still empty - the experience being removed. Same discipline as "invoke a dialog after every event-container write".
- **The notice must never write to the EVENT container.** It polls via `@st.fragment(run_every=1.0)`, and a fragment rerun **rewinds that container's write index** - so a style-only `st.html` or an `st.toast` inside it would land on a neighbouring stylesheet's host and strip a component of its CSS. Its stylesheet therefore lives in the login page's **unconditional** block (a conditional one shifts every later host by one), and the fragment emits exactly ONE element in every state.
- **The resolved token is NOT consumed - found by driving the real app, not by the tests.** The unlock state is process-global (a background thread has no `ScriptRunContext`) while `keychain_unlock_pending` is per SESSION, so a second session - a reload, or a second window - set pending, found the unlock already `ok`, received an **empty** token because session one had taken it, and landed on a bare login page with no notice. Keeping the value costs nothing (it is already in the Keychain and in session one's `api_token`) and `reset_keychain_unlock()` clears it whenever the credential is dropped, at **both** the logout and `force_reauth` sites.
- **`store_token` is deliberately NOT given the same treatment.** Its pre-read exists to *skip a write that cannot change anything*, and that skip is what stops a refused write destroying the credential (`set_password` on macOS is delete-then-add). Making that read non-interactive would trade a safe prompt for a **destructive** write, because the write's own delete prompts anyway. So a user who denies and then pastes a fresh token still meets one prompt at login - contextual, user-initiated, and bounded. Stated rather than fixed.
- Verified end to end in the real app: post-update prompt -> **painted login page + notice in 1.12 s**; the user clicks Always Allow -> the app **signs itself in with no app interaction**; next launch -> **1.15 s, no notice, no dialog**, and the suppressed read confirms the ACL really was updated.
- Covered by `tests/test_keychain_unlock.py` (33) and `scripts/_mutate_keychain_unlock.py` - **23/23 caught**, so re-run the mutation pass rather than just the suite. **Two survivors in the first pass were real gaps in my own tests**, and both are worth remembering: counting keychain READS is not a test of single-flight (the workers serialise on the UI lock, so exactly one read happens either way - count **THREADS**), and nothing checked `app.py`'s call site at all, so deleting the adoption call while leaving `from ui.auth import adopt_pending_keychain_unlock` above it satisfied every test.

### A mutation pass needs a per-mutant TIMEOUT, or a hanging mutant strands itself
This file already warns that a concurrent commit can capture a mutant. The same damage arrives from the pass's own runtime: a mutant that makes the code **block** (here, the probe taking the UI lock blocking, deadlocked against a test that holds it) hangs pytest, the pass is eventually killed from outside, and its `finally` never runs - so the **mutant is left on disk**, indistinguishable from real code. That happened while this set was being written and was caught only by grepping the source for `if False:` afterwards. `subprocess.run(..., timeout=...)` bounds it and a timeout counts as CAUGHT (a mutant that hangs the suite is one the suite noticed); the runner also asserts the restore actually took. The other half is the test's own duty, which this repo already states for the ffmpeg watchdog: **a test for "this does not block" must run the call on a thread with a join timeout, so a regression FAILS instead of hanging.**

## Never destroy data on the strength of an error you have not IDENTIFIED (2026-08-06)
The failure audit found the same mistake in both of the app's stores, each time in the code written to *recover* from damage. Neither language gives you the distinction structurally, so both had to be made explicit. `tests/test_failure_hardening.py` guards all of it; all **15** mutations of the real code are caught, so re-run the mutation pass, not just the suite, after touching any of it.
- **`sqlite3.OperationalError` IS a `sqlite3.DatabaseError`**, so `except sqlite3.DatabaseError` caught *transient* failures too - and `_init_db_locked`'s handler renamed the manifest to `.canvas_sync_corrupted.db`, or **deleted** it when the rename failed. Three purely transient conditions arrive as `OperationalError` (all verified): `unable to open database file` (share offline, disk full, permissions, a malformed path), `database is locked` (another process, antivirus, a stuck WAL), `attempt to write a readonly database`. **Reproduced: a folder left with NO manifest and NO backup**, from an error whose file on disk was never damaged - attempt 0 renamed the DB aside, attempt 1 unlinked that very backup and then failed to rename again. Losing the manifest is not cosmetic: every local file becomes untracked, so the next sync re-downloads over the student's annotated copies, and `_NewVersion` protection cannot fire because it reads manifest rows.
  - **Corruption must now be PROVEN, by message whitelist** (`_is_db_corruption`: "file is not a database", "database disk image is malformed", "integrity check failed", …). An unrecognised error is treated as transient **on purpose** - the cost is asymmetric: a transient error misread as corruption destroys the sync record, while corruption misread as transient only shows an error and asks the user to retry. Transient → bounded retry → `_db_init_failed = True`, file untouched.
  - **The delete-on-rename-failure fallback is gone.** A corrupt manifest can still be salvaged by hand; a deleted one cannot. Backups are never overwritten either - the FIRST one is the copy closest to the last good sync, so new ones take `.canvas_sync_corrupted_2.db` and up.
