- **The guard is a per-mutant snapshot comparison, not a second `_clean()`**: before applying each mutant the source must still equal the snapshot taken at the start, or the pass ABORTS (rc 3) naming the file and the mutant.
- **On abort it restores NOTHING, and that is the whole point.** The check runs BEFORE the write and the previous mutant was already restored, so no mutant is on disk - the only thing there is somebody else's edit, and writing the snapshot over it is exactly the data loss being prevented. **The first version of the guard called `_restore()` on the abort path and destroyed the edit anyway**; the control is what caught it, not review.
- **Restore is from the snapshot, not from git.** `git checkout --` depends on HEAD still being what the pass started from (which the 2026-08-10 incident shows it may not be) and rewrites every target whether or not the pass touched it.
- A control has to edit the file **in the gap between two mutants** - waiting for it to go mutated, then back to original. Writing at t=0 is caught by the clean check instead, which is a different guard; the first version of that control did exactly that and reported a failure that was not one.

## Deep crash/leak audit (2026-08-08): every finding was a class this file ALREADY documents
Twelve defects, and not one was a new kind of mistake - each was a place an earlier sweep did not reach. That is the reusable result: **when a bug class is found, the fix is not done until the class has been swept across every module that could hold it.** Covered by `tests/test_crash_vector_hardening.py`; all **54** mutations of the real code are caught, so re-run the mutation pass, not just the suite. (Two sweeps came back genuinely clean and are worth not repeating: platform guards - every `windll`/`win32com`/`osascript` site is lazy-imported and guarded; and regex backtracking - 0 nested-quantifier patterns. `win11toast` was also cleared: it sets `text.inner_text` through the WinRT **DOM**, which escapes, so a course named "Marketing & Sales" cannot break a toast.)

- **"Unreadable" is not "empty" - the SETTINGS file had the bug the sync stores were fixed for.** `core/library.py` and `atomic_update_sync_pairs` were hardened against `load → degrade to {} → save`; `canvas_downloader_settings.json` was never swept with them, and TWO handlers did exactly that (the Settings dialog's save, and the login success path). One unreadable read replaced every key the handler does not itself set - **`panopto_notice_ack_version`, i.e. an accepted legal notice, the whole `panopto` engine block, `default_download_path`, `show_help_text`**. Reachable three ways, none hypothetical: a `UnicodeDecodeError` from a file re-saved in a Danish ANSI codepage (this file already cites that for the sync configs), a `JSONDecodeError` from a file damaged by the pre-atomic legacy writers, and a transient `OSError` (antivirus lock, config dir on an offline share). The irony is that the dialog's own comment said "which is exactly why it must not be rewritten as a fresh dict" - directly above the `except` that did it.
  - `ui/auth.py:read_config_for_update() -> (config, may_write)` is the one reader, split by CAUSE like its precedents: **damaged content** is quarantined to `*.corrupt.json` and a fresh write is allowed; a **transient `OSError`** returns `may_write=False` and the caller must not write. The two callers differ on purpose - the dialog reports "could not save, try again", while **login skips the write silently and logs in anyway**, because a settings write must never be able to block a login (the token is already in the keyring).
  - Same class, two smaller instances: `core/health_log.session_end` wiped the snapshot holding this session's pid and recorded children (so the next launch had nothing to reap and the orphans leaked for good), and `_save_fallback_token` rebuilt the DPAPI store from scratch, **logging the user out of every OTHER saved Canvas**.
  - **An existing test caught the refactor and was right to** - `test_settings_save_is_read_modify_write` grepped for a literal `json.load`. Re-anchored on the behaviour, not the spelling, and extended to cover the new verdict; same "brittle anchor reads like a missing guard" trap this file already documents.

- **An Office COM process spawned but never reclaimed.** `_init_app` calls `DispatchEx` and then sets properties. **Word set `DisplayAlerts` and Excel set `Visible`/`DisplayAlerts`/`EnableEvents` unguarded** - while the PowerPoint sibling wrapped the identical calls with the comment *"Some Office 365 builds restrict these flags"*. On such a build the raise landed in a handler that did `self.app = None`, and `self._com_pid` was **still unset because the PID was captured AFTER the properties**. The instance was then unreachable from both directions: no COM reference to `Quit()`, no PID to kill, and **the session orphan reaper cannot see it either - a COM-spawned Office is a child of DCOM/RPCSS, not of us**. One orphaned `WINWORD.EXE`/`EXCEL.EXE` per conversion attempt, for the life of the session.
  - Three changes, all three files: capture the PID **immediately** after `DispatchEx` (`find_new_office_pid` returns on its first poll, so it is free), guard every property set, and make the failure handler call `_kill_app()` instead of dropping the reference. The PID capture is itself guarded, so a failed lookup leaves a *usable* instance rather than an abandoned one. A test asserts the ORDERING by AST - everything between `DispatchEx` and the PID capture is a window where a raise strands a real process.

- **A background worker's "running" claim must be released if the thread cannot start.** `core/course_cache.fetch_courses` already guards `Thread.start()` raising `RuntimeError`; `panopto/models.start_download` and `panopto/cuda_provision.start_provision` both set a "running" state and THEN started the only thing that would ever clear it. The state pinned for the life of the process: a progress bar that cannot move, and - worse - each function's own is-running guard then **refuses every retry**, so the user can never install the model or the CUDA libraries again without restarting the app. `_download_worker` had a second door to the same room: its import, key lookup and `target.mkdir()` sat ABOVE the `try`, so a read-only volume or full disk killed the thread with the state still reading "downloading".

- **One path key, not three.** Three places ask "is this file on disk already tracked?" by comparing a MANIFEST path against an `os.walk` path, and they had three different normalisations - `heal_manifest` and the untracked-file count used bare `normpath`, `analyze_course` used `normcase(normpath(...))`, and **none applied Unicode normalisation**. Same divergent-primitive shape as the `make_long_path` duplicate. `core/sync_manager._path_key` is now the one primitive, `normcase(normpath(NFC(s)))`:
  - **Case** - a case-only rename (`Notes.pdf` → `notes.pdf`) is legal and invisible on Windows/macOS, but the raw strings stop matching. Measured: the tracked file drops out of the set, so it is offered to the healer as an orphan **and inflates the "untracked files" count the review screen shows** - a count whose stated purpose is to match what the user sees in the folder.
  - **Unicode** - **APFS is normalisation-PRESERVING, so a modern Mac hands back the NFC that was written and is unaffected. HFS+ is not** - it stores the NFD form (Unicode 3.2), so `os.walk` returns decomposed names there. That is an external drive, which is an ordinary place to keep a course folder. Danish **`å` decomposes, `ø` and `æ` do not** (nor do they on `é ü ö ã ñ`, which all do) - which is precisely why the symptom would read as random rather than as an encoding bug. Not verifiable from Windows; the fix is a no-op on every platform where the two forms already agree.

- **`_is_partial_artifact` missed `.tmp`.** Two writers put their temp file INSIDE the course folder rather than the config dir - `shared.shortcuts.write_shortcut` (`<name>.url.tmp`, one per Panopto Shortcut output) and `converters.url` (`Compiled_External_Links.tmp`) - so a crash between the write and the `os.replace` strands one exactly where the analyzer looks. Unrecognised, it read as study material **and was eligible to be healed onto a genuinely missing manifest row**, pointing that row at a half-written file.

- **macOS: `_as_posix` did not escape line breaks.** It is the shared helper the module docstring tells every converter to use for "a POSIX path safe for embedding in an AppleScript string literal", and it escaped `\` and `"` but not `\n`/`\r`. An AppleScript string literal cannot span lines, so a raw newline is a SYNTAX error that kills the whole script - every Office conversion in that folder fails with an opaque osascript message. macOS permits every byte but `/` and NUL in a filename, so it is reachable from the user's own chosen folder and from an **extracted archive member** (zip member names never pass through `_sanitize_filename`). Not an injection - `"` is still escaped, so nothing can close the string - but a silent, total failure. `shared/helpers.native_folder_picker` was already escaping newlines in its own hand-built AppleScript; the shared helper was simply weaker than the one ad-hoc site.

- **A recursive walk over SERVER data needs a bound - and the file already knew that.** `_build_discussion_replies_html_sync.render_entry` recursed through Canvas discussion replies with `depth` used **only for the visual indent** (`min(depth*30, 150)`), never as a limit - while its sibling forty lines away, `humanize_canvas_error._messages`, is depth-capped and says why: *"an adversarial or merely odd payload must not be able to raise RecursionError out of an exception handler"*. Two costs, and the second is the one that matters: `entry.get_replies()` is a **network call**, so the recursion is unbounded work driven by the other end (the same shape as the pagination loops above); and a cyclic reply graph recursed until `RecursionError`, which the enclosing `except Exception` swallows into `""` - **the entire Replies section vanishing from the export with no error anywhere**. Now capped at `_DISCUSSION_MAX_REPLY_DEPTH` (30, against a renderer whose indent already saturates at depth 5) with a `seen` set on entry id, mirroring the Panopto folder walk. Measured against the real function: a 500-deep chain made 30 calls instead of 500, a self-referencing reply terminated on the first repeat, and a normal 3-level thread renders unchanged. **The truncation is announced in the export** - it is the user's only copy of that thread, so it must say what is missing rather than silently ending.

- **`converters/md.py` was the last weak sibling in the delete family** - found by the SAME counting sweep, one round later. `code.py` checks `st_size`, `video.py` and the Office trio call into `converters/verify.py`; `md.py` checked a bare `exists()`, and a 0-byte file exists. `markdownify` returns `""` for a page whose visible content is all script/style - which this converter has *just decomposed itself* - so a Canvas Page rendered by an embedded widget, an LTI iframe or an H5P embed produced an empty `.md` **and lost its source HTML**. Reproduced against the real converter on four page shapes.
  - Two guards, because they answer different questions: `md_content.strip()` **before** the write (did the conversion extract anything?) so a content-free page leaves the folder untouched instead of gaining an empty `.md` the next sync has to reason about; and `file_has_content` **after** it (did the bytes reach disk?), which is the gate the rest of the family already uses and whose own docstring names this exact moment.
  - **A census beats reading, and the census is now a test.** `test_every_source_deleting_converter_verifies_content_not_just_existence` asserts every source-deleting converter CALLS a content gate - matched on the *call*, not the token, because a leftover `import file_has_content` from a reverted fix satisfies a substring test while nothing runs. That refinement was itself forced by a surviving mutation.

- **macOS deleted the user's original Office file on an UNVERIFIED PDF** - the single most consequential finding of this audit, and a pure platform asymmetry: the Windows branch of all three Office converters had been hardened with `pdf_looks_real`, the macOS branch of all three had not. Full detail in "An Office converter deletes the user's original" above.

- **A listing loop must not trust the SERVER to end it.** All three pagination loops in `panopto/discovery.py` advanced a page counter and exited only when the server returned a SHORT page - so termination was the other end's to honour. A tenant (or a caching proxy) that ignores the page parameter and re-serves page 0 loops **for ever, on the Streamlit script thread where discovery runs**, so there is no Cancel to press, while the results list grows by a full page each iteration with nothing bounding the memory. Driven with a fake that never stops: all three ran unbounded before, all three stop at the cap now.
  - **The asymmetry is inside ONE function.** `_discover_folder_sessions` bounds its own folder walk carefully - a `seen` set against cycles, `depth <= 3`, `<= 40 folders` - and its docstring advertises "(bounded: depth <= 3, <= 40 folders)". The per-folder page loops it calls **up to 40 times** had no bound at all. The two Panopto loops are themselves asymmetric: `_folder_sessions_data_svc` has a secondary bound (`len(found) >= TotalNumber`) and `_folder_sessions_api_v1` has none - and the secondary one only fires when the server sends `TotalNumber`, which the surrounding code already anticipates being absent.
  - `_MAX_LIST_PAGES` is 100, i.e. 10,000 sessions in a SINGLE folder - orders of magnitude past anything real (the largest course folder measured here held 36), so a legitimate listing can never reach it. A test pins the floor, because a cap tight enough to truncate real data is a correctness bug of its own.
  - **The termination tests run on a THREAD with a join timeout**, so a regression FAILS instead of hanging the suite - the same lesson `panopto/stream.py`'s watchdog test already records.

- **A naive/aware datetime comparison is a TypeError, and it sat OUTSIDE the try written to catch it.** `_is_canvas_newer` parses two timestamp strings in a `try ... except (ValueError, TypeError)` and then compared them on the next line, *after* the try - so the one exception the handler names is the one it could not catch. Nothing above catches it either: **`analyze_course` has ZERO try blocks around that call** (checked by AST), so it aborts the whole course's analysis. Both strings are server-controlled and never validated, and `fromisoformat` happily parses an offset-less `2026-07-01T10:00:00` or a bare `2026-07-01` as NAIVE. Reproduced against the real function in all three mixed shapes. **Reachability is not proven** - Canvas normally stamps the trailing `Z` - so this is hardening of an unvalidated input rather than a fix for an observed failure; that is also why the fix NORMALISES rather than just widening the guard. A naive value is read as UTC (what Canvas documents), so the verdict is **right** instead of merely safe: falling into the handler would answer "not updated" and silently skip a genuine update. Normalisation only ever FILLS a missing offset - a real one is honoured, and the mutation pass pins that (overwriting a real `+02:00` is caught, as is normalising either side to the wrong zone).

- **Both transcription sidecar handles must be opened INSIDE the try.** `txt_f` and `srt_f` were opened above it, so a failure on the SECOND (disk full, permissions, an antivirus hold) left the first handle open with the try not yet entered - the `finally` never ran. Not academic on Windows: **the traceback keeps the frame, and therefore the handle, alive for as long as Streamlit displays it**, so the partial `<name>.txt.part` stays locked and cannot be cleaned up or rewritten.

- **`proc.wait()` after the transcription worker's stdout EOF was unbounded.** That line is reached on a `break` out of the read loop, and one of those breaks is stdout **EOF - which means the PIPE closed, not that the worker exited**. The `finally` that kills the child only runs after `wait()` returns, so there was nothing to rescue it, and the daily auto-sync runs unattended with no user to cancel. Bounded at `_EXIT_GRACE_SECONDS` (10) with a kill fallback. **This is NOT the stall watchdog that was deliberately declined for transcription** (see above): that one would have to bound the whole transcribe, where slow and wedged are genuinely hard to tell apart; by this line the worker has finished producing output and only process teardown remains. A test pins the constant small enough that it cannot quietly become one.

## The settings file is CO-OWNED by four modules, and three of them erased it (2026-08-09)
The 2026-08-08 sweep fixed `load → degrade to {} → save` on `canvas_downloader_settings.json` **in `ui/auth.py` and stopped there**. That file is written by four modules, and the other three still did exactly what the section above describes - which is the playbook's whole thesis restated: the productive question is never "what could be wrong" but *"which module did the last fix for this class NOT reach?"*
- **The writers were `panopto.settings.save_settings`, `panopto.settings.set_globally_enabled` and `shared.legal.record_panopto_acknowledgement`.** Each did `full = _read_full_config()` → mutate one key → write, and `_read_full_config()` returned `{}` on ANY exception. Reproduced against the real functions: a 7-key settings file became a **1-key** file, and **every writer returned `True`**. Lost: `panopto_notice_ack_version` (an accepted LEGAL NOTICE - the user is asked to accept it again), the whole `panopto` engine block, `canvas_url`, `default_download_path`, `show_help_text`, `use_12h_format`. All three docstrings claimed the opposite ("so the other top-level keys ... are never clobbered") - the same false promise the Settings dialog's comment carried before it was fixed.
- **The remedy is ONE primitive, `shared.helpers.read_json_for_update(path) -> (data, may_write)`**, and `ui/auth.py` now delegates to it too. Two implementations of one decision is what produced this bug in the first place - the identical lesson `make_long_path` already taught (a second copy in `core/sync_manager.py` meant the fix reached none of the 26 manifest call sites). The **path is a parameter**, not resolved inside, so `ui/auth.py`'s import-time `CONFIG_FILE` tempdir fallback stays its own business; `_migrate_config` also stays in `ui/auth.py`, because moving a legacy token into the keyring must not happen as a side effect of another module saving its settings block.
- **Split by CAUSE, and the two halves differ on purpose**: *damaged content* (malformed JSON, or bytes that are not UTF-8 - `UnicodeDecodeError` is a SIBLING of `JSONDecodeError`, both `ValueError`) is quarantined and the write PROCEEDS, so the user gets a working file back; a *transient `OSError`* returns `may_write=False` and the caller writes NOTHING, because nothing is wrong with the file. A non-dict payload counts as damaged - callers subscript the result.
- **`core.today_store` had the same shape**, one level quieter: `load_today_config()` degraded to defaults and `_update()` persisted them, so an unreadable `today_dashboard.json` silently set `auto_sync_enabled: False`. The daily sync is the run nobody watches, so nothing would have said so.
- **`core.preset_manager` was the last unswept JSON store, and it held BOTH defects.** `UnicodeDecodeError` escaped `load_presets` entirely (caught neither by its `except json.JSONDecodeError` nor its `except IOError`) and propagated into the Presets hub, which calls it **unguarded inside an `@st.dialog`** - where a raise blanks the modal. Preset names are typed by the user, so `æøå` is ordinary. And its transient branch returned `[]` while `save_preset`/`delete_preset` do load → mutate → save, so one antivirus lock replaced the user's whole preset library with the single preset they were adding. `save_preset` now returns **`None`** rather than raising (the call site has no handler - same rule as `write_config_atomically`: a persistence failure must be REPORTED, never allowed to abort the user's action) and `ui/presets.py` says so instead of toasting success.
- **What was deliberately NOT changed there**: this module keeps its own quarantine policy - the `.corrupt` suffix, and replacing a stale backup so the NEWEST corrupt copy survives. Three existing tests pin it, and it is defensible *for this file*: after a corruption the store is rebuilt from empty, so the newer copy holds the more recent presets, whereas the sync manifest keeps the FIRST copy because that one is closest to the last good sync. Different files, different lifecycles, not a contradiction. Only the "last resort: delete the corrupt file" branch was dropped - a corrupt presets file can still be salvaged by hand, and saving works again regardless because `_save_all_locked` rewrites the whole file.
- Covered by `tests/test_settings_coownership.py` (10) and `tests/test_preset_store_hardening.py` (14). **A transient-failure test must make the READ fail while the WRITE still succeeds** - the first version put a directory where the file goes, which fails `os.replace` too, so the guard could be deleted with the test still green: **four mutants survived** on that alone. Patch `open` for reads of that one path instead. `tests/test_settings_coownership.py:_assert_isolated` also refuses to run a writer unless `get_config_dir()` resolves to the test's own tmp dir - without it, a `monkeypatch.undo()` that reverts the fixture's `CANVAS_DL_CONFIG_DIR` silently rewrites **the developer's real settings file in the repo root**, which is exactly what happened while that file was being written.

## The pre-launch offline hardening pass (2026-08-11): three defects, every one a class already in this file
Run on the macOS audit machine AFTER the live audit was finished, so the live oracles had nothing left to say. Every finding came from a MECHANICAL sweep of a class this file already documents, in the module the previous fix did not reach - the playbook's thesis for the third pass running. The suite was green on macOS throughout (3,430 -> 3,487), Rule 4 through Rule 10 stayed at 0 violations, and each fix was reproduced against the real function, mutation-verified, and followed by a full-suite re-run.

### The sync HISTORY was the store the "unreadable is not empty" sweep never reached
Six stores had been fixed for `load -> degrade -> save` (`core.library`, `core.library_migrate`, `core.preset_manager`, `core.today_store`, `ui.auth`, `atomic_update_sync_pairs`). `canvas_sync_history.json` was not one of them, and it is the complete record of every sync in both modes - the Sync page's history, the Today page's lens over that same record, and the `course_names` that `shared/components._course_id_from_sync_pairs` matches error rows against.
- **Measured against the real class: 50 seeded runs became 1.** `load_history()` degraded EVERY failure to `[]` - correct for the two DISPLAY surfaces, which is exactly what made it look safe - and BOTH mutators then did `history = load_history()` -> append/amend -> atomic write. One transient read (antivirus lock, config dir on a share briefly offline, a permissions blip) replaced the record with the single entry being added.
- **`amend_last_entry` was the same bug in disguise, and that is the part worth remembering.** It answered `False` on an empty history and its own docstring says the caller then "creates one" - so a transient failure funnelled straight into the `add_entry` that does the damage. It now answers **True** ("handled, change nothing"). A GENUINELY empty history must still answer `False`, because that answer is what makes the caller create the entry; a guard that swallows the real case would be a second bug, so both directions are pinned.
- **The cause split REUSES `shared.helpers.read_json_for_update`** rather than restating it, generalised with an `expect` type that defaults to `dict` so every pre-existing caller is untouched. History is a LIST; asking for the dict default would quarantine every valid history file as "wrong shape". That primitive's own docstring already explains why a second implementation is the mistake - `make_long_path`'s copy in `core/sync_manager.py` reached none of the 26 manifest call sites.
- **The display readers stay total and now LOG.** Rendering `[]` tells the user "you have no sync history", the identical confusion this file already records for `_render_sync_history`.
- Swept the last store with the shape: `engine/applescript_bridge`'s macOS Office permission record, where a transient read dropped the OTHER apps' answered Automation prompts and would silently re-launch Word, Excel and PowerPoint on a later run. `core/health_log.py` and `panopto/cuda_provision.py` were checked and are clean (the first already refuses to write on an unreadable snapshot; the second's reads are read-only, written wholesale).
- `tests/test_sync_history_hardening.py` (26); all **11** mutations caught.

### A near-MISS between two literal sets is what a divergent primitive looks like as it drifts
Scanning for identical duplicated collections finds the known-and-deliberate ones. Scanning for sets that overlap **>=60% but are NOT identical** finds the live bug: the LTI-stream verdict.
- **The MESSAGE and the ERROR TYPE had been unified into `shared/helpers.py` long ago; the PREDICATE that chooses them was left behind as two copies** - `core/canvas_logic.py` (download) and `sync/execution.py` (sync) - and **both omitted `.m4v`**, while the size-mismatch tolerance in the very same download engine lists `.m4v` as media. One engine disagreed with **itself**.
- **The consequence is a screen that lies about whose fault it is.** A URL-less Canvas file with a media extension is a plugin STREAM - a permanent fact about the course that no retry, setting or wait can change - and the completion screen classifies `LTI_STREAM_REASON` to keep it out of the failure count. An `.m4v` stream fell through to the generic branch, so an otherwise-clean run rendered as "Completed with Errors", the colour that is supposed to mean "look at this".
- `shared.helpers.is_lti_stream_ext` now lives beside the two constants it selects, and both engines ask it. **The neighbouring size-mismatch list is deliberately NOT merged and a test pins that**: it holds the same members today but answers a different question - "may a short read be tolerated?" versus "is this a stream?" - and this file already carries the `.pdf`-in-`file_in_scope` example of what merging two same-looking lists costs.
- `tests/test_lti_stream_classification.py` (29); all **8** mutations caught - but only after **two of my own tests were exposed as weak by the pass itself**, which is the reusable part: one fed `LTI_STREAM_REASON` straight to the downstream classifier and therefore **passed with the bug still present** (the classifier reads the MESSAGE; the defect was upstream, in deciding to produce it), and one grepped for the token `flexible_extensions`, which a mutation aliasing the shared set to that very name satisfied. Assert the **binding** and the **producer branch**, via AST.

### A GENERATED file that is also CHECKED IN ships whatever was last committed
`version_info.py` (the Windows PE version resource) is generated by `scripts/build_windows.py`, is tracked in git, and is consumed directly by the spec (`version='version_info.py'`). The Build section of this file documents the Windows build as a bare `pyinstaller Canvas_Downloader.spec`, which does **not** regenerate it - so it declared `2.0.1` while `version.py` said `2.0.2`, and every v2.0.2 exe would have reported the previous release in Explorer's file properties. It does not reach the in-app update banner (that reads `version.__version__`), which is precisely why nothing caught it. Now regenerated, with a test that fails when the two disagree **and** a second that fails if the spec stops consuming the file - without which the first would be measuring nothing.

### Sweeps that came back CLEAN on real macOS - do not repeat them
Each was mechanical, whole-project, or driven against the real machine, so a future hit is genuinely new:
- **Filename sanitiser fuzz against the real filesystem**: 450 hostile inputs (traversal, reserved device names, NUL/CR/LF, bidi overrides, 400-char names, emoji, Danish, URL-encoded) -> **0 directory escapes, 0 write failures, 0 name-too-long**. Note APFS limits to **255 characters, not bytes** (`128 x 'æ'` = 256 bytes is accepted), and the sanitiser's own 120-char cap sits well inside it.
  - **CORRECTED 2026-08-20: the component limit is 255 UTF-16 CODE UNITS**, not bytes and not characters. The line above said "255 characters, not bytes" and `MAC_RUNBOOK.md` said "255 bytes" - two documents, two readings, both wrong. Measured by writing real files on APFS: `255 x æ` (510 BYTES) is legal, `128 x emoji` (128 CHARACTERS) is not, and `253 x æ + 1 emoji` (255 units) is legal while one more `æ` is not. APFS stores names as UTF-16 and an astral character is a surrogate pair; **NTFS counts the same way**, so this is not a platform-specific guard.
  - **The hazard is a UNIT MISMATCH, not a value.** `_sanitize_filename`'s cap is in CHARACTERS (`max_length=120`) and the filesystem's is in UNITS, at a worst-case ratio of 2:1 - so the safe cap is **127**, and the measured worst output is **236 units against a 255 ceiling**, a margin of about ten emoji. Raising the cap to preserve longer Canvas names - an ordinary request - breaks only all-astral filenames, only past 127, and fails as ENAMETOOLONG **at download time, i.e. as a missing file**. `tests/test_sanitize_filename.py` derives the bound from the signature's own default rather than restating 120, so the test cannot go stale against the constant it guards; `scripts/_mutate_filename_utf16_cap.py`, 4/4 caught.
  - **`office_safe_path` is a no-op off Windows** (documented, with a comment), so macOS long-path safety comes entirely from `office_container_stage`'s short `src_<hex>` staging. Verified live: **442-char and 900-char paths both convert** to a real `%PDF-` at the long destination. Going further needs more directory LEVELS, not a longer name.
- **macOS `PATH_MAX` is 1024 and there is NO `\\?\` escape hatch** (`make_long_path` is a documented no-op off Windows), so a deep enough tree really does raise `ENAMETOOLONG`. The degradation is correct: every one of the seven `asyncio.gather` sites passes `return_exceptions=True` **and** inspects the results, turning one over-long path into one reported file rather than a lost course.
- **Silent broad handlers**: 426 exist, but the large ones are all either reporting (the two ~250/440-line sync-engine handlers append to `error_list`, write a terminal line and log a full traceback via `log_debug_exc`) or documented best-effort (`count_course_items`). No new instance of the "swallowed hook" shape.
- **Cleanup-after-loop that a `BaseException` would skip** (the class behind the `.part` sidecar leak): the one real instance is already in a `finally`; the rest are thread `join`s and in-memory `openpyxl` closes.
- **Packaged bundle**: `codesign --verify --deep --strict` rc=0, `apple-events` entitlement intact, `pync` gone. Gatekeeper **rejects** it (ad-hoc, unnotarized) as expected, and the docs are correctly version-branched - macOS 13/14 right-click->Open, macOS 15+ System Settings -> Privacy & Security -> Open Anyway, which is right because Sequoia removed the Control-click bypass. **A quarantined nested helper still executes**, so the transcription worker is not blocked on first run.
- **The real app on this Mac**: boots headless, login page renders with 0 `stException` and 0 page errors, and the institution picker answers accent-folded queries correctly (`kobenhavn` -> Københavns Universitet, `harvard` -> Harvard University first, `erhvervsakademi` -> Erhvervsakademi Aarhus).

### Known, NOT verified - stated rather than guessed
*(iCloud eviction used to be the headline entry here. It is MEASURED as of
2026-08-20 - see "iCloud 'Optimize Mac Storage'" below.)*

**A leftover harness can impersonate the app, and it nearly cost a wrong conclusion.** Three streamlit processes from earlier sessions were still listening on 8599/8601/8603/8605. Launching the app on 8599 failed with "Port 8599 is already in use", the health check still answered 200, and the browser rendered the *completion-screen gallery* - which read exactly like "the app boots clean". **Always confirm the listener's PID is yours** (`lsof -nP -iTCP:<port> -sTCP:LISTEN`) before believing anything a local port tells you.

## A re-download REWROTE the edit-protection baseline - one bookkeeping line (2026-08-20)
`original_md5` means **what we downloaded**. It is the sole basis of the app's
headline promise: `_classify_local_modification` compares the file on disk
against it to answer `clean` (safe to overwrite) or `modified` (preserve as
`_NewVersion`). `record_downloaded_file` rewrote it from whatever was on disk.

- **Found while measuring something else.** The iCloud pass asked "does a
  re-download materialise an evicted folder?" - it did, 19 of 19 - and chasing
  the cause landed on a line whose *other* effect is data loss. The materialise
  question was the symptom; this is the disease.
- **`record_downloaded_file` runs after EVERY file of a download, including the
  ones that were SKIPPED because they already existed** (`core/canvas_logic.py`,
  *"Sync Run #0: Record skipped-but-existing files to the DB"*), and that call
  site passes no md5. The function then hashed the file on disk and stored it -
  so a re-download replaced the baseline with the file's CURRENT content.
  Measured, driving the real class:

      first download        baseline = md5(original bytes)
      student edits it      classification -> 'modified'   (protected)
      re-download course    baseline = md5(THE EDIT)
                            classification -> 'clean'      (NOT protected)

  `clean` is the verdict that lets the next sync overwrite the file, and
  `_NewVersion` cannot fire because it reads this row.
- **The edit must preserve the file's SIZE**, because a size change is what
  sends the download down the overwrite branch instead of the skip branch. That
  makes it narrow - and silent, and aimed at the one thing the product promises
  about the user's own work.
- **The same line was the certain iCloud cost**: hashing READS the file, and
  reading an evicted file materialises it. Re-running a download over a folder
  macOS had evicted pulled the whole course back - **19 of 19** untouched files,
  against **0 of 22** for the sync path, measured on a real account.
- **`clear_ignored` already was the fresh-vs-skip discriminator** and its
  docstring already said so, so the fix needed no new state: a caller with no
  md5 is either recording bytes it just WROTE (`clear_ignored=True` - secondary
  HTML/URL renders, where the file is ours and hashing is right) or re-recording
  a skip-existing file (where it is NOT ours). The stored baseline is preferred
  whenever one exists; hashing stays the fallback for a row that has none, which
  keeps the original promise that a baseline is never silently dropped.
- **Verified end to end on the real folder**: after the fix a real download over
  a fully evicted iCloud course materialised **0** files. The one that changed
  blocks was the `.webloc`, which `_create_link` REWRITES every run - our own
  write resetting its blocks, not a fetch (confirmed by its mtime).
- `tests/test_download_baseline_preservation.py` (8) and
  `scripts/_mutate_download_baseline.py` - **4/4 caught, plus one documented
  EQUIVALENT** mutant (trusting an empty stored baseline changes nothing,
  because the fallback hashes on any falsy md5; the `and _prev[0]` guard is
  belt-and-braces). The mutation pass is what exposed a genuine gap in my own
  tests first - a row that is MISSING and a row that EXISTS WITH AN EMPTY md5
  reach different branches, and only the second distinguishes them.

## iCloud "Optimize Mac Storage": SUPPORTED, and now pinned by tests (2026-08-20)
A student on a cheap small-SSD Mac is a core persona, and macOS EVICTS their
course files to free space: the name and size stay, the bytes do not
(`st_blocks == 0`), and **the first process to READ one silently downloads it
again**. So on such a folder hashing a file is not cheap - it is a network fetch
and a disk refill, and it undoes the very setting the student turned on.

**Measured on macOS 26.6.1 against a real iCloud account, driving the REAL
`SyncManager`** (an account was created on the audit box for this; the two
previous runs had none, which is the only reason this stayed open):

    analysis, nothing changed on Canvas    0 of 10 files materialised
    analysis, ONE genuine update           1 of 12   (only the changed one)
    heal_manifest after a RENAME           1 of 12   (only the renamed one)
    os.replace onto a dataless target      works, content correct
    read of a dataless file                correct md5 in ~0.9-1.35 s -> 'clean'

- **macOS 26 uses DATALESS FILES, not `.icloud` placeholder stubs.** The listing
  after eviction shows the real names and nothing else, so an evicted file does
  NOT read as a missing file plus an untracked stub - the failure that would
  have made every sync re-download the course. (Not verified on macOS 13/14.)
- **The engine passes because of three properties nothing was pinning**: the
  folder walks take `stat().st_size` and never open a file; candidate md5s are
  explicitly lazy (`'md5': None  # Lazy compute`); and the update path hashes
  only what `_is_canvas_newer` already selected. A reasonable refactor - *"just
  hash everything up front, it's simpler"* - would silently turn every sync into
  a full re-download for these users, **and no existing test would have failed**.
  `tests/test_icloud_dataless.py` is now that contract, and it is PORTABLE: it
  counts calls to `compute_local_md5` rather than needing iCloud, so it holds
  the line on Windows and in CI, where the failure it prevents is invisible.
- **Hashing the updated file is REQUIRED and must not be "optimised" away.** It
  is how an edited local copy is told from a clean one, which is what
  `_NewVersion` rests on - and a dataless file can absolutely contain the user's
  edits, because edits sync to iCloud and are then evicted. The materialisation
  is the price of the edit-protection guarantee. What must never happen is
  hashing its NEIGHBOURS.
- **A materialisation that FAILS is safe by construction.** `compute_local_md5`
  catches `OSError` broadly - which is what matters, since a failed fetch
  surfaces as `EIO`/`ETIMEDOUT` and not `PermissionError` - returns `None`, and
  `_classify_local_modification` biases to `'modified'`, i.e. `_NewVersion`.
  The local copy is preserved; the cost is a sibling, never an overwrite.
  Narrowing that handler would let the error escape into `analyze_course`, which
  has NO try around the call and would abort the whole course.
- **`_is_canvas_newer` treats same-size-newer-timestamp as a metadata touch**
  and returns False. A probe that "changes" a file by bumping only its timestamp
  therefore measures nothing - the first version of this measurement fooled
  itself exactly that way and nearly recorded "0 materialised on an update" as a
  good result. A test fixture here must change the SIZE.
- **Detect dataless with `st_blocks == 0` at a nonzero `st_size`.** Do NOT use
  `brctl status`: on a freshly-enabled account it returns *"Client zone not
  found"* for a path that is plainly there, which reads like a broken test. That
  is brctl failing to QUERY, not the file being unsynced - proved by evicting a
  file (blocks 248 -> 0) and reading the correct md5 back.
- 5/5 mutations caught (`scripts/_mutate_icloud_dataless.py`), each a plausible
  refactor rather than a strawman. Re-run the pass, not just the suite.

**Still unmeasured**: a real materialisation FAILURE (offline, or an account
over quota). Only its consequence is measured, above. Cutting the network is not
available on a machine reached over remote desktop - it strands the operator and
the agent both.


## Quit only what WE launched - asking the documents cannot answer it (2026-08-12)
Found by driving all three Office apps in the ordinary *"the user is editing while a sync converts"* state. **Word closed the user's document and quit; Excel and PowerPoint did not** - which is exactly why this needed all three to surface.
- **The defaults on a failed property read were the data loss.** `_idle_quit_script` calls a document the user's if it has a path on disk or unsaved changes, each read in its own `try`, defaulting to `hasPath=false` / `isSaved=true` - **which is precisely the definition of PRISTINE**, so an undescribable document did not block and the quit goes out `saving no`. The docstring claimed the opposite ("defaults to blocker … never to a wrong quit").
- **And the documents ARE undescribable.** Measured: before a conversion phase `path=[Macintosh HD:…:MY WORD WORK.doc] saved=false`; after one, **name / path / full name / saved ALL FAIL**. Not stale references - the reads fail inside a single `tell` block. Every app is left holding one such document, and `_force_close_canvas_docs_sync` cannot close it either, because it identifies documents by those same properties.
- **NEITHER default works, and that is the point.** Undescribable-as-pristine destroys a student's unsaved essay; undescribable-as-blocker left all three apps in the dock after every run (measured: exactly that, plus no Recents purge, since the purge declines while an app runs).
- **So stop asking the documents and ask who launched the app** - the rule the product owner chose. An app the user already had open is never even asked to quit; an app WE launched holds only our documents, so an undescribable one there is ours. Stated explicitly as `undescribable_is_ours` rather than left as an accident of the defaults.
- **THE OBSERVATION MUST BE TAKEN AT THE TOP OF `prime_office_automation`, AND THIS IS THE PART A HARNESS CANNOT CATCH.** Priming launches every enabled app with `open -g -j` at run start, so by the time the first conversion asks "was it already running?", **all three are running by our own hand** - the gate then calls them all the user's and quits nothing, which is the exact opposite failure to the one it was added for. Measured in the real app with all three killed beforehand: *"PowerPoint was already running before this run"*, all three left in the dock, 70 of our entries stranded in Recents. Every harness in this repo passed, because they drive the converters directly and never prime. The call in `run_applescript` stays as an idempotent fallback for those flows. `_APP_TRIPLES` (priming) and `_APP_DOC_MAP` (the gate) must keep naming the apps identically or the gate silently stops protecting one; a test pins that.
- **Verified in the REAL APP, both states, course 43660, with converted PDFs deleted first so genuine conversions had to run**: *cold* - `quit sent (1 open doc(s), none user-owned)` for all three, 8/8 re-converted, all quit, Recents 0; *busy* - `left alone (the user already had it open)` for all three, 6/6 re-converted, **all three user documents intact**, 0 failures.
- Second defect, same lean: the `full name` fallback tested `contains "/"`, but Word reports an **HFS colon path**, so it could never fire for Word.
- Verified end to end on all three apps (`scripts/verify_office_end_to_end.py`): **cold** 12/12 converted, all three quit, Recents 37 -> 0; **busy** 12/12 converted, **3/3 user documents survived**, all three left running. `tests/test_office_quit_scoping.py`; all 6 mutations caught.

## …and the observation is PER RUN, taken at EVERY launcher (2026-08-13)
The fix above is only as good as the moment its observation is taken, and that moment was recorded in **one of the three places that can launch an Office app, once per PROCESS**. Three holes, each reproduced against the real functions from Windows with `sys.platform` reporting darwin. This is the same class the section above closed, one level up - which is the playbook's thesis for the fifth pass running: *the productive question is never "what could be wrong" but "which module did the last fix for this class NOT reach?"*
- **`first_run_permission_setup` is a launcher too, and it is the EARLIER one.** It spawns the same `_warmup_apps` (`open -g -j`) and never observed. Both `app.py` and `sync/execution.py` call it at **run start**, reaching `prime_office_automation` only per course, later - so on a machine whose Automation grants are not yet recorded, i.e. **a new user's FIRST run**, the batch launched all three, the later observation saw our own launch, every app read as the user's, **nothing was quit, and the Recents purge declined as well** (it refuses while every app is running). Measured: `alive after the batch: [Excel, PowerPoint, Word]` -> `apps this run will QUIT: NONE`. The D11 symptom, on the run that forms a first impression.
- **`reset_office_priming` cleared every other piece of per-run Office state and not this one**, so run 2 of a session answered with run 1's facts. The dangerous direction is the reachable one: run 1 launches Word and records "ours"; the user then opens Word and starts an unsaved essay; run 2 still reads "ours" and the teardown quits it `saving no`. Run 2 has just had a conversion phase - **the exact state this file records Word's documents as undescribable in** - so the document check, the only remaining defence, cannot see it either. `_office_quit_fired` *is* reset per run by both callers, so the teardown really does fire again. The opposite direction (a stale "theirs") only ever costs an app left in the dock, which is why the fix is to **re-observe** rather than to age or reconcile the value.
- **"Never observed" is a THIRD answer, not a False.** `office_was_preexisting` was `dict.get(app, False)`, so an app we never drove counted as ours - reachable by cancelling before priming ran, and **the teardown fires on the cancelled screens too**. `office_is_ours_to_quit` is now `_office_preexisting.get(app) is False`: observed-and-not-running → quit, observed-and-running → leave, **absent → leave**. That is the correct reading and not merely the safe one; `test_an_unobserved_app_is_not_treated_as_the_users` said so in its own docstring while asserting the opposite, and the behaviour won.
- **The duty sits on `_warmup_apps`, the ONE function that actually launches**, so a future fourth caller is safe by construction rather than by remembering - the same move as `run_applescript` wrapping `_run_applescript_locked` so the lock cannot be forgotten. Both callers *also* observe on their own (calling) thread, because `_warmup_apps` runs on a worker and thread scheduling must not decide correctness. `observe_office_before_launch()` covers **all three apps, not just the ones being launched**: a run's contract widens between courses (`office_contract_from_folder` is per folder), so the app nobody was going to open is exactly the one launched later.
- **`_note_office_preexisting` is double-checked under a lock, first writer wins.** Two callers straddling a launch could both pass an unlocked "not recorded yet" check and let the LATER, post-launch answer win - the one direction that costs a document. The lock is contended at most once per app per run; the fast path never takes it.
- **The teardown STATES the undescribable policy instead of inheriting it.** `_idle_quit_script`'s `undescribable_is_ours` defaulted to **True** while its single call site passed nothing, so the docstring's "the caller passes True only for an app IT LAUNCHED" described a contract nothing enforced. It now defaults to **False** and the teardown passes `True` explicitly, one line under the gate that earns it.
- **A test counts the SITES, not the fix**: `test_EVERY_function_that_launches_an_office_app_observes_first` walks the module for everything that reaches `_warmup_apps` and asserts each observes first. The 2026-08-12 test checked `prime_office_automation` because that is where the fix landed - the same shape as `pdf_looks_real`, written for two delete sites and landing on one for eight months.
- **A harness cannot catch this and did not**: every scenario here drives the converters directly, so the launchers never run. The reproduction was `sys.platform` patched to darwin with `pgrep` and the launcher modelled - which proves the DECISION path only; the "documents become undescribable" half is the 2026-08-12 macOS measurement, not re-measured. **STILL UNVERIFIED ON A MAC**, and filed as such: `MAC_RUNBOOK.md` Phase M1 **step 8** is the procedure, `AUDIT_FINDINGS.md` fp `989c128a238d` is the open work item, and `MAC_OFFICE_FIXES.md` known gap 5 is the honest list. The check that matters is **two runs in ONE session with a dirty document opened in between** - `pkill`ing between them is not a substitute, because the bug is about state inside one process, which is exactly why `scripts/verify_office_end_to_end.py` (cold/busy, one run each) cannot see it as written.
- **The residual case, considered and DECLINED**: the predicate asks "was it running when we looked?", not "did we drive it?", and `_QUIT_TARGETS` is all three apps every run - so an app observed as idle and never touched still counts as ours, and a user opening it MID-RUN is asked to quit. The document check protects them there, soundly: no conversion phase ran for that app, so its documents are still describable and only a pristine blank is quit. A separate "we actually drove it" set would close it exactly, and is a SECOND fact about the same question kept in a second place - which is the bug this file has now fixed twice.
### …and it RAN on a Mac at last - the document check has a THRESHOLD (2026-08-20)
The gate above shipped verified only by a Windows simulation, and the register
said so (`fp:989c128a238d`, HIGH). It now runs on macOS 26.6.1, and the half the
simulation could not reach turns out to be **load-dependent** - which is what
makes a small test pass while proving nothing.
- **All three Phase M1 step 8 checks pass against the real applications**: first
  run (both apps quit, Recents 4 -> 0), **two runs in ONE process with a dirty
  unsaved Word document opened between them** (`left alone (we did not launch
  it)`, document intact), and cancel (`left alone (we never drove it this
  run)`). The two "left alone" reasons are told apart on real hardware, which is
  the distinction D11 was about.
- **A conversion phase leaves Word's documents undescribable only past THREE
  files.** Measured, reading `name`/`path`/`full name`/`saved` after a real
  phase with the user's document open: **2 files -> all four read correctly; 3,
  4 and 6 -> all four FAIL.** Below the threshold the document check still
  works, so it catches a wrong gate decision and nothing looks broken.
- **So a harness that converts one or two files is STRUCTURALLY BLIND to this**
  - the same shape as D11 passing every harness in the repo, one variable
  further in. The first 8(b) run of this session used `--files 2` to save rented
  time, printed `VERDICT: ALL GOOD`, and armed nothing; re-run at `--files 4` it
  passes for the right reason. `scripts/verify_office_end_to_end.py:
  MIN_ARMING_FILES` (3) now REFUSES a below-threshold run, naming why.
- **The negative control is what proves the gate is the SOLE defence**, and it
  needs no mutation: send `_idle_quit_script(..., undescribable_is_ours=True)` -
  byte-for-byte what the teardown emits for an app it believes it launched -
  after a 6-file phase with the user's dirty document open. It answers `quit
  sent (1 open doc(s), none user-owned)`, Word quits, the document is closed.
- **Probe traps**: `repeat with d in documents` INSIDE a `tell application`
  block dies `-1708` ("every document doesn't understand the count message") -
  the rule is already written in `_idle_quit_script`, so read the product's
  idiom rather than inventing one. And building a `.pptx` by driving PowerPoint
  (`make new presentation` + `save`) times out `-1712` and crashes it into
  Microsoft Error Reporting, because the save panel is hidden under `open -g -j`.

- **Modelling `pgrep` in a test: sample the process table at CALL time, then delay.** Sampling after the delay models a probe that sees the future, and the thread-race test then fails against correct code - which is how the first version of it "found" a defect that was not there.
- `tests/test_office_quit_scoping.py` (27); all **17** mutations of the real code are caught. Note that removing EITHER idempotence guard alone is an equivalent mutant now the function is double-checked - the mutant has to span both.

## The Recents purge deleted VALUES and left every entry on screen (2026-08-12)
The function the app relies on to clean up after itself had never removed a single Recents entry.
- Office's start-screen Recent list is driven by rows in `HKEY_CURRENT_USER`, each a node whose **`name` IS the full `file://` URL**. The old code selected `HKEY_CURRENT_USER_values WHERE name='path'` and deleted only value rows: measured on the real registry, **495 value rows removed, 0 nodes** - every entry still listed, now stripped of its values. The whole database held **36** rows named `path`. A half-mutation of Office's SHARED registry is worse than not running.
- Now matched on the node NAME, deleting node + values, behind **three gates**: the marker (a directory name we own), **leaf-only** (so it can never amputate a subtree), and an **`MruUserData` ancestor**. Verified on the real database: **636 removed, 0 of the user's 1041 nodes touched, 0.04 s**.
- **Per APP, not all-or-nothing.** Our entries split PowerPoint 487 / Excel 135 / Word 14, each under `MruUserData/UnsignedUser/<App>/Local/Documents`, so a run cleans the apps that are closed while the user keeps editing in the third. An entry belonging to a RUNNING app is left alone - it would be resurrected from that app's in-memory list on exit - and an entry that cannot be attributed to an app is only taken when nothing is running.
- **The purge lands ~15 s AFTER the last process dies** (wait-for-exit plus the retry pass), and it runs on a daemon thread. Anything measuring the Recents list before that reports a working purge as broken - which cost one debugging cycle here.
- `tests/test_office_recents_purge.py`; 9/10 mutations caught, the survivor documented as equivalent (with every app running the per-app gates already refuse everything, so the early return is only an optimisation).

## macOS gives a user session ONE PowerPoint - two app instances crash it (2026-08-11)
**This is the root cause of the reported "a powerpoint somehow crashed" and of the crash in the 2026-08-11 download matrix**, which ran two audit lanes, i.e. two app instances. Reproduced ON DEMAND by starting two conversion batches at the same moment against the real applications.
- **A conversion is `open` -> `save active <doc>` -> `close`, and that is INDIVISIBLE.** There is one Microsoft PowerPoint / Word / Excel per macOS user session, so a second instance's `open` lands between our `open` and our `save` - and we then save and close ITS document. Measured: **batch A 0/8, batch B 0/8**, `Connection is invalid. (-609)`, "reported success but no output file was created", a stray **`B8 (1).pdf`** where two conversions raced for one destination, and PowerPoint crashed into Microsoft Error Reporting.
- **THE STAGED BASENAME MUST BE UNIQUE, and that is why.** It was the constant `src.<ext>` with the uuid only in the DIRECTORY - so `our_document_test`, which identifies our document by NAME, answered "yes, mine" for the other instance's document. The guard written that same morning for the data-loss defect was blind to exactly this case: **`guard_trips` was 0 while all 16 files failed**. The token is 6 hex of the work dir, keeping the basename at 15 bytes so Word's ~255-byte staged-path limit is untouched.
- **`run_applescript` holds a per-app `flock` for ONE conversion** - the indivisible unit, not the whole phase, so a second instance waits seconds rather than a whole course. **`flock` specifically, because the kernel releases it when the holder dies**: a crashed instance is the very thing this defends against and must not be able to strand every later run. Bounded (120 s) then proceeds anyway - degrading to the OLD behaviour, never to a stalled run. Per app, so Word never waits on PowerPoint.
- **`start.py`'s single-instance guard is not enough on its own** - this file already records that it **fails OPEN by design** in three ways. A defence whose failure mode is a crashed Office app and a folder of half-converted lectures needs a second layer.
- After both fixes the identical concurrent run gives **8/8 and 8/8**, no stray PDFs, PowerPoint alive, batch B taking 14.2 s against A's 7.7 s - the lock doing its job. `tests/test_office_concurrency.py`; 8/9 mutations caught, the survivor documented as equivalent (`fh.close()` releases a flock, so an explicit `LOCK_UN` is belt-and-braces).
- **Measured and deliberately NOT shipped**: a polling version of the document guard, in case `open` returned before the document became frontmost under load. It does not - **0 polls needed in 10/10 opens, cold and under full 10-core load** (`scripts/probe_office_open_latency.py`). Nor is the 448 MB lecture deck in course 43660 the culprit: it converts in 3.0 s at 245 MB peak RSS. Do not re-add machinery for either.

## An EMPTY case-sensitive volume answered "case-insensitive" (2026-08-12)
`_probe_case_insensitive` flips the case of a CHILD entry, because a directory's own name lives on its PARENT volume and at a mount point those are two different filesystems. That has one blind spot it cannot cover: **a directory with no child to flip**, where the code fell back to flipping the directory's own name.
- Measured against a real case-sensitive APFS image (`hdiutil create -fs "Case-sensitive APFS"`), probed while still EMPTY: the fallback flipped `/Volumes/CDCaseSens`, resolved it against `/Volumes` on the case-INSENSITIVE root volume, and **returned True for a case-SENSITIVE drive**. The comment claimed the fallback "answers False on doubt"; at a mount point it answered True, which is the **MERGING** direction - `_path_key` lower-cases, so `Notes.pdf` and `notes.pdf` collapse to one manifest row and a heal mis-binds.
- **Reachable**: `_case_insensitive_volume` climbs to the nearest EXISTING ancestor, so a course folder that does not exist yet on a freshly-formatted external drive resolves to the drive's empty root. `_probe_case_insensitive` is `lru_cache`d, so the wrong answer is then frozen for the session even after the first files land.
- The fallback now refuses at a mount point (`os.path.ismount`). Verified on both image types: sensitive answers False at every stage and keeps the two names apart; insensitive answers True once the folder holds a file, and folds them. **False on an empty case-INSENSITIVE root is the recoverable direction** and is what "False on doubt" always meant.
- Related, verified on a real READ-ONLY mount and needing no change: a read-only volume produces `unable to open database file`, which is retried three times and then **leaves the manifest untouched** with a user-facing RuntimeError naming the likely causes. No rename, no delete, no `.canvas_sync_corrupted.db`.

## `active document` is whoever is FRONTMOST - the Office converters trusted it (2026-08-11)
Found by the macOS 26.6 audit. All three converters bound the document they were about to export with a bare `active presentation` / `active document` / `active workbook`, and their `on error` handler did `close active <doc> saving no`. Full detail in `tests/audit/MAC_OFFICE_FIXES.md`; the durable rules:
- **A failed conversion DISCARDED the user's unsaved edits.** Measured with a user document open and dirty, then a corrupt file fed to the real converter: **pre-fix 1 -> 0 documents, fixed 1 -> 1**, with the CONTROL converting a real PDF in both (so the guard did not simply stop converting). The success path was the silent half: a slow `open`, or an app in crash-recovery with a recovered deck frontmost, exports THEIR document into our PDF and then closes it.
- **The guard is `name of active <klass>`, and that is MEASURED, not chosen.** Every other binding form fails somewhere: capturing `open`'s result is -2753 in all three apps; `whose name is` returns NOT FOUND in **Excel**; `<klass> "name"` and both `repeat` forms **HUNG** PowerPoint or Excel outright. Only the name-of-active comparison works in all three, and it is the only one that never ENUMERATES - which is the point, because two of the enumerating forms wedged the app hard enough that **Microsoft Error Reporting offered to restart it**, i.e. the enumeration would have CAUSED the window-flashing symptom it was meant to fix. `office_container_stage` stages as `src.<ext>` in a uuid dir, so that name is one we own.
- **It fails SAFE in the direction that matters**: if the frontmost document is not ours we touch nothing, so the worst case is a document of ours left open (the teardown force-closes marker-matched documents) instead of a user document closed without saving.
- **Excel demonstrated the live path while the fix was being verified**: handed a corrupt file it opens nothing at all, leaving the USER'S workbook frontmost - and the old code closed that. Its `page setup` now comes from `active sheet of theBook` too, or a mis-bound workbook would have its layout rewritten.
- `tests/test_office_document_guard.py`; all **15** mutations caught. The tests COUNT the guard sites per converter, because this repo's history is that a fix lands on two of three and nobody notices for months (`pdf_looks_real`, Windows-only, for eight months).

## `-600` means the app CRASHED, not that it is missing (2026-08-11)
Three log lines, three seconds apart, on the download matrix: `Parameter error. (-50)` -> `Application isn't running. (-600)` -> `skipping remaining 57 PowerPoint file(s)`.
- **`-600` is `procNotFound`** - "not running *right now*", exactly the state a crash leaves - and the next `tell application` relaunches the app. It was classified `app_missing`, which is in `FATAL_CATEGORIES`, so **57 files were abandoned for the rest of the run** and the user was told an app they had just watched convert forty files "is not installed or could not be launched".
- It now has its own `app_crashed` category: per-file, **retried once** after a short pause (macOS must reap the dead process, and MER takes its turn first), and NOT fatal. An unrecoverable crash still ends the phase through `SYSTEMIC_REPEAT_THRESHOLD` after three consecutive failures - the mechanism that can actually tell "one bad deck" from "the app is gone". Genuine absence (`-10810`, `-10814`) is untouched and still fatal, because retrying an app that is not installed only delays an honest error.
- **Office writes a TYPOGRAPHIC apostrophe**, so the pre-existing `"isn't running"` test (straight quote) never matched and the whole classification rested on the `-600` substring. Both forms are covered now, each with its own no-error-number test - the first version had only the curly one plus a straight-apostrophe message that also carried `-600`, and the mutation pass caught it.
- **The WORDING clauses are locale-fragile, and three of the four were dead - measured on macOS 26.6.1 (2026-08-20).** The `-600` fix above gave `isn't running` both apostrophe forms and **stopped there**; its neighbours in the same function kept only the ASCII one, and macOS writes a TYPOGRAPHIC apostrophe. Made osascript fail for real:
  - **`app_missing` was the live defect.** A genuinely absent app says `Can't get application "X". (-1728)` with U+2019, so the clause never fired - and unlike `permission` there is **no numeric companion** (-1728 is absent from the list, deliberately, see below). The verdict fell through to `other`, which is not in `FATAL_CATEGORIES`. So a user without Microsoft Office - not a free product, so an ordinary state - never sees *"Word is not installed"*: they get three generic per-file errors and then the systemic message telling them to **"Quit Microsoft Word and run again"**, about an app they do not have.
  - **`permission` was dead too and nobody could tell**, because `-1743` carried it: this machine's macOS emits the **British** `Not authorised to send Apple events` and the clause knew only `authorized`. A locale can change the words; it cannot change the number.
  - **Normalise the apostrophe ONCE, up front** - not two spellings per clause. Spelling each clause twice is precisely what produced this: one clause had both forms, its siblings had one, and the difference is invisible in review. A clause added later now cannot inherit the bug.
  - **`-1728` must NOT go in the numeric list, and that is the non-obvious half.** It is `errAENoSuchObject`, which OUR OWN scripts raise for an absent DOCUMENT (`can't get active document`) - mapping the code wholesale would abort a phase with *"Office is not installed"* on a machine where it plainly is, i.e. a fatal, and the wrong one. The wording separates the two exactly, which is why the apostrophe had to be fixed rather than the number added; a mutant pins it as *"the obvious fix, and wrong"*.
  - **Known remaining gap, stated rather than closed**: on a Danish-language macOS every wording clause matches nothing and only the numeric codes decide. That is why `app_missing` losing its number matters more than it looks.
- `tests/test_office_crash_is_not_missing.py` (29); `scripts/_mutate_office_guard.py crash` **8/8** and `scripts/_mutate_applescript_locale.py` **6/6**. The crash set was 10 until the two apostrophe-CLAUSE mutants were retired - the property they guarded moved to the locale set, and `tests/test_mutation_anchors.py` failed them in the same commit that moved the code, which is exactly what it exists for.

## The staged product is promoted only if it is REAL (2026-08-11)
`office_container_stage`'s exit promoted anything that EXISTED, under a comment calling it the "success path". A conversion that errors part-way still leaves whatever Office had written.
- Measured: an **870-byte "PDF"** in course 43660 beside the `.pptx` it failed to convert - tracked by nothing, so re-offered as NEW on every future sync for ever. **And `dst.unlink()` runs first**, so a failed re-conversion DESTROYED the good PDF a previous run had produced. The folder went backwards.
- The gate is the same `converters.verify` pair every source-deleting converter already applies afterwards, placed **at the promotion** - the boundary all three converters cross, so a fourth gets it free. Asking in both places is not redundant: this decides what the user's FOLDER gains, the converter's decides whether the ORIGINAL may be deleted.
- **The no-container path is asymmetric on purpose**: a reject that created a new file is removed; a reject that OVERWROTE something is KEPT and reported, because deleting it would turn a damaged file into a missing one and a manifest row may point there.
- Same class as `converters/archive.py:_decline`, in the module that fix did not reach. `tests/test_office_product_gate.py`; all **9** mutations caught.

## The macOS 15 live audit (2026-08-10): five product defects, and the class each belongs to
Every one was invisible on Windows and invisible in the unit suite, and four of the five were found by driving the REAL app on real hardware. The register (`tests/audit/AUDIT_FINDINGS.md`) has the evidence; these are the rules.

- **A FAILED Keychain save DESTROYS the token that was already saved.** macOS's keyring backend deletes any existing item before adding the new one, so a refused write leaves *nothing*. Measured: a stored value, one `set_password` raising `PasswordSetError (-25308)`, and the item reads back `None`. Three things then hid it - `_safe_keyring_set` **returns False rather than raising**, so the login flow's `except Exception` amber notice was unreachable; `_save_fallback_token` is a deliberate no-op off Windows; and the only trace was a `logger.warning` claiming it had been *"Saved to DPAPI-encrypted fallback storage"*, which on macOS is nothing at all. The user's saved login vanished, the next launch showed the login page, and nothing on screen ever said why. **`ui.auth.store_token` is now the ONE writer**: skip a write that cannot change anything (the common path, and the one that turns a refused write into data loss), write, fall back, then **VERIFY by reading back** - so its return value describes the STORED STATE, not one call's return code, which is what makes it safe for a caller to delete its own copy. Both legacy-migration sites now pop the JSON token **only on True**; that is the one run where the JSON copy is the only copy.
  - **The reads use a SHORT watchdog (`_KEYRING_PROBE_TIMEOUT`), the write keeps the full 90s.** macOS can PROMPT on a keychain access - a rebuilt app with a new ad-hoc signature reading the previous build's item, hit on the audit machine - and the prompt blocks. Two full-budget calls would have turned one 90-second login stall into two. Verified in the packaged app's own log: `set_password timed out (90s)` followed by `get_password timed out (5s)`.
- **Office container staging must use a SHORT basename.** `office_container_stage` staged as `work / src.name`, i.e. it shortened the DIRECTORY - which was never the problem, since it discards the user's directory anyway - and preserved the NAME, which was. Word's limit is the **~255-character TOTAL path** and the container prefix costs a measured **91**, so the effective filename budget was ~164 bytes: **any Canvas file named past that could not be converted at all**, silently, per file. Canvas reaches it easily - a lecture title with the course code and week runs well past 100 characters. Now `src.<ext>` / `out.<ext>`; the real name is only needed at the final destination, which the function already moves the product to. A 240-byte name converts.
  - The discriminating measurement, because every other one fits both hypotheses: hold the name at 9 bytes and deepen the staged directory - staged ~220 **converts**, staged ~281 **fails**. And **the unstaged path is NOT a usable control**: with no container macOS demands the per-folder App Data grant staging exists to avoid, so even a short-named control fails there. An intermediate draft of this analysis concluded "component, not total path" from exactly that unsound comparison.
  - `active document doesn't understand the "save as" message` (Word opened it) and `missing value doesn't understand …` (Word did not) are **different** signals; the second is the wedge. Any Office test needs a **fresh Word and its own positive control per case**, or it measures the previous case's wedge.
- **`os.path.normcase` is the IDENTITY off Windows, and the macOS default volume is case-INSENSITIVE** (probed: home APFS and `/tmp`). So `_path_key`'s documented case half did nothing on the platform where `Notes.pdf` and `notes.pdf` really are one file - and one physical file then counted as an untracked orphan AND a missing row, inflating the number the review screen exists to reconcile. Folding unconditionally is the trap: a case-SENSITIVE volume (an ordinary external drive) holds both as two files, and folding there mis-binds a heal. Gated on `_case_insensitive_volume()` - read-only (`samefile` against the case-flipped path, no probe file), `lru_cache`d, climbing to the nearest existing ancestor, **False on any doubt**, which is the previous behaviour exactly. Verified in both directions on REAL volumes, including a case-sensitive APFS image made with `hdiutil`.
- **`version.py` not being bumped is a user-visible defect, not bookkeeping.** `ui/update_banner.py` computes `_is_newer(github_tag, __version__)`, so tagging a release while the bundle says the previous version tells **every user, on every launch, for ever** that an update is available - pointing at the build they are running. `tests/test_version_leads_tags.py` now fails when `version.py` does not lead every shipped tag (skips when no tags are visible, since a shallow clone is not evidence).
- **An idempotence guard only holds if EVERY path goes through it.** `start.py`'s shutdown was a `_shutdown()` behind a `threading.Event`, and the comment said the window-close route therefore closes the health record once - but the `finally` called `session_end()` and `_terminate_child_processes()` directly, bypassing it. Measured in the packaged app: **two identical `SESSION END (clean)` lines** for one START. The `finally` routes through `_shutdown` now.

### The pair being EDITED was identified by its position in the list (2026-08-11)
Investigated on a hunch that `pending_sync_folder` "smelled like" the other identity bugs, and it **proved out**: `editing_pair_idx` is an index into `st.session_state['sync_pairs']`, and the list moves under an open edit form, because the form replaces only its OWN row while every other row keeps a live **Remove** button (and a remove-all path exists). Four positional uses, three of them wrong in a way the user cannot see:
- **The save.** `old_pair = sync_pairs[edit_idx]` decided which pair to repoint, and then `_retarget_saved_pair_lazy` moved that pair's user-given NAME to the new link. `[A,B,C]` edit C, remove A -> `0 <= 2 < 2` is false, so Save Changes fell into the append branch and **created a duplicate pair out of an edit**. `[A,B,C,D]` edit C, remove A -> index 2 is now **D**, so Save Changes **repointed D at C's chosen folder/course and moved D's name**, leaving C untouched.
- **The duplicate check** excluded "self" with `if i != editing_idx`, so after a shift it excluded the wrong pair - which both hides a real duplicate and can block a legitimate save with *"this pair is already on your sync list"*.
- **The mismatch warning** compared against `sync_pairs[editing_idx]`, so it suppressed or invented the course/folder warning for the wrong pair.
- All four now resolve through **`core.pair_labels.pair_key`** / object identity. A NEW session key `editing_pair_sig` carries the link; `editing_pair_idx` survives only as the "a form is open" flag that a dozen `editing_idx is None` checks already read.
- **A vanished pair must CLOSE the form, not fall through.** The old append branch turned a lost edit into a silent duplicate; the form now refuses with a sentence naming what happened. And the render gate matches on the LINK, so an open form **follows its pair** when the list shifts instead of binding to a stranger.
- **THE TRAP MY OWN FIX INTRODUCED, caught by a test rather than by reading**: two "Add Course" buttons popped `editing_pair_idx` and not the new sig. Once the form is matched on the link, a surviving sig makes *Add Course* open the **edit** form on the previously-edited row. `editing_pair_sig` must be cleared wherever `editing_pair_idx` is - `tests/test_ui_leak_and_pair_scoping.py` counts the clear sites in each file and fails when the two disagree, which is the only reason this was found.
- Covered by that file (39 tests); all **7** mutations caught, so re-run the mutation pass rather than just the suite.

### A folder's FILE FILTER decides what the analyzer may even consider (2026-08-11)
Reported as *"seventy six new files, and they have not been added to the course since I downloaded it right before"*. Reproduced from the folder's own manifest and debug log, and it was permanent rather than cosmetic - the same 76 on every sync, for ever.
- **The mismatch**: the download engine skips Canvas **Pages** and module **links** outright under the "Slides & PDFs" filter (`file_filter == 'study'`), while `_get_files_from_modules` - the scan that feeds the sync ANALYZER - enumerated them unconditionally. So the diff compared Canvas against a manifest that could never contain them. Evidence from course 43660: `sync_contract file_filter: "study"`, `secondary_content_contract` all false **and correctly honoured** (`enabled=[none]` in the log), `download_mode flat`, **0** manifest rows for `.html`/`.webloc`, on disk 56 pdf + 51 pptx + 11 pptm and no html/webloc at all, log `Analysis complete: 76 new | 0 clean updates`. Accepting them would have turned the folder into a shape the user never configured.
- **THE RULE WAS WRITTEN IN SIX PLACES AND THE SIXTH DISAGREED** - three branches in `download_course_async`'s module walk, two in the folder-structure walk, one in the metadata scan. `core.canvas_logic.module_item_in_scope(item_type, file_filter)` is now the only definition and all six ask it; a test COUNTS the call sites, because a fix that lands on five of six is invisible in review. The first pass here found only three of them.
- **OUT OF SCOPE IS NOT IGNORED, and that distinction is the design.** `is_ignored` records a decision the USER made; these were never offered to them. Out-of-scope items are dropped before the diff and appear nowhere - not as new, not as ignored. The alternative (mark them ignored) floods the count with 30-70 entries the user never chose against and destroys the meaning of the one number they rely on.
- **The scan's `file_filter` defaults to `'all'`, deliberately permissive**: a folder with no stored contract may show MORE than it should, never silently hide a file. A corrupt contract falls back the same way rather than raising mid-analysis.
- **DECIDED (product owner, 2026-08-11): one folder, one configuration, no widening.** There is no UI to change a folder's scope after the fact - the two filtered modes are deliberate, uncommon choices, and a re-configuration flow is complexity for a fraction of users. Re-download if you want a different shape.
- **RESOLVED the same day by the section below** - which also found that the missing files half was not merely untidy but was actively widening filtered folders. Read that section, not this line.

### The FILES half of the same rule, and it was widening folders one file at a time (2026-08-11)
The paragraph above closed with "`analyze_course` never applies `file_filter` to Files-tab files at all", filed as a tidiness debt whose only cost was 23 mislabelled rows. It was not. Driving the real analyzer showed the same defect as the Pages/links half, live and permanent:
- **A file uploaded to Canvas AFTER the initial download has no `is_ignored` stub, so it was offered as NEW** - and **`sync/execution.py` contains no file filter at all**, so selecting it downloaded it. Measured: a new `Groupwork.xlsx` in a `study` folder came back in `new_files`. A "Slides & PDFs" folder therefore drifted toward All-Files one lecturer upload at a time, silently, which is precisely the shape the product owner ruled against ("one folder, one configuration").
- **The stubs themselves were reported as IGNORED and the dialog offered to RESTORE them** - 23 in a real folder - a decision the user was never asked to make, behind a button that could not do what it said (the analyzer would decline to produce the file).
- **`file_in_scope(disk_name, file_filter)` is the FILES counterpart of `module_item_in_scope`**, and the allowlist existed by hand in **five** places: the download gate, `count_course_items`, `get_course_total_size_mb`, Quick Sync's own re-filter, and `app.py`'s scan.
